# Physics — Answers

Questions: [questions.md](questions.md). Answer numbers match question numbers.

## VCE Units 1 & 2

### Unit 1: Thermal physics

#### Temperature and the kinetic particle model

**A1.** D. 310 K
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-1 -->

Explanation: T(K) = T(°C) + 273 = 37 + 273 = 310 K. Subtracting 273 gives 236 K, which is a common conversion error.

**A2.** B. average random kinetic energy of its particles
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-2 -->

Explanation: Temperature is proportional to the average random (translational) kinetic energy of the particles. Total internal energy also depends on the amount of substance and on potential energy.

**A3.** D. The particles move faster, so they hit the walls more often and with greater force
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-3 -->

Explanation: Higher temperature means greater average speed, so collisions with the walls are more frequent and each exerts a greater force. The volume and number of particles are unchanged.

**A4.** D. X and Y have the same temperature but Y has greater internal energy
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-4 -->

Explanation: Both samples have the same average particle kinetic energy, so the same temperature. Y contains twice as many particles, so its total internal energy is greater.

**A5.** C. 4 times larger and the average speed 2 times larger
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-5 -->

Explanation: Average kinetic energy is proportional to T, so it increases by 1200/300 = 4. Since KE = ½mv², speed increases by √4 = 2.

**A6.** (a) 77 − 273 = −196 °C; (b) 20 + 273 = 293 K; (c) 293 − 77 = 216 K (a difference of 216 K is the same as 216 °C).
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A7.** Temperature is a measure of the average random kinetic energy of the particles; internal energy is the total of the kinetic and potential energies of all the particles; example: a bathtub of water and a cup of water at the same temperature have the same average particle KE but the bathtub has far greater internal energy because it has many more particles.
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A8.** Heating increases the average kinetic energy (and speed) of the air particles; particles collide with the can walls more frequently; each collision exerts a greater force (greater change in momentum); the volume and number of particles are constant so the pressure increases.
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A9.** C. 273 K
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-9 -->

Explanation: T(K) = T(°C) + 273, so 0 °C = 273 K. A value of 0 K is absolute zero, not the freezing point of water.

**A10.** B. −273 °C
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-10 -->

Explanation: T(°C) = T(K) − 273 = 0 − 273 = −273 °C. The value −373 °C comes from wrongly subtracting 100 as well.

**A11.** A. 25 °C
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-11 -->

Explanation: A kelvin and a degree Celsius are the same size, so a change of 25 K is a change of 25 °C. Adding 273 applies only when converting a temperature, not a difference.

**A12.** D. bombarded unevenly by fast-moving, randomly moving water molecules
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-12 -->

Explanation: Water molecules are in constant random motion and their collisions with a grain do not balance exactly at every instant, giving a net random push. This is Brownian motion and is evidence for particle motion.

**A13.** A. the same average kinetic energy but a greater average speed
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-13 -->

Explanation: At the same temperature the average translational kinetic energy is the same for all gases. Since KE = ½mv², the lighter hydrogen molecules must move faster on average.

**A14.** C. have the minimum possible random kinetic energy
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-14 -->

Explanation: Temperature measures average random kinetic energy, so 0 K corresponds to the lowest possible random kinetic energy, and no lower temperature exists. Melting and freezing occur at different, substance-dependent temperatures.

**A15.** D. doubles
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-15 -->

Explanation: In kelvin the temperature rises from 300 K to 600 K, and average kinetic energy is proportional to T in kelvin, so it doubles. Using the Celsius values gives the incorrect factor of about 12.

**A16.** A. average random kinetic energy per particle
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-16 -->

Explanation: Thermal equilibrium means equal temperature, which means equal average random kinetic energy per particle. Equal speeds would only follow if the particles had equal mass, and total internal energy depends on the amount of substance.

**A17.** C. from the cup to the bath, because the cup has the higher temperature
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-17 -->

Explanation: Net energy transfer is from the higher-temperature body to the lower-temperature body, because particles with greater average kinetic energy lose energy in collisions. The bath's greater total internal energy is irrelevant to the direction.

**A18.** B. the particles hit the walls more often, since they have less distance to travel between collisions
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-18 -->

Explanation: At constant temperature the average speed, and so the force per collision, is unchanged. In a smaller volume the particles strike the walls more frequently, so the pressure rises.

**A19.** D. There are negligible forces between the particles except during collisions
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-19 -->

Explanation: An ideal gas has particles that are very small compared with their separation and interact only through brief, elastic collisions. Attractive forces at all separations or energy loss at each collision are not assumed.

**A20.** A. They vibrate about their fixed positions with a greater average kinetic energy
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-20 -->

Explanation: In a solid the particles are held in fixed positions and vibrate. Raising the temperature increases the average kinetic energy, and so the vibration, without letting the particles move freely, which would require melting.

**A21.** C. 0 °C at the freezing point and 100 °C at the boiling point of water at standard atmospheric pressure
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-21 -->

Explanation: The Celsius scale is defined using the freezing and boiling points of water at standard atmospheric pressure, with equal divisions between. Absolute zero is the basis of the kelvin scale, and body temperature is not a fixed point.

**A22.** B. The particles move randomly in all directions, so on average equal numbers collide with each wall
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-22 -->

Explanation: Particle motion is random, with no preferred direction, so each wall receives the same average collision rate and force. Gravity has a negligible effect on the particles of a gas.

**A23.** B. 313 °C
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-23 -->

Explanation: Average kinetic energy is proportional to the kelvin temperature: 293 K × 2 = 586 K, which is 586 − 273 = 313 °C. Doubling the Celsius value to 40 °C is a common error, and 586 is the kelvin value, not Celsius.

**A24.** C. 1.7
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-24 -->

Explanation: Kinetic energy is proportional to T, so it rises by 900/300 = 3. Speed is proportional to the square root of that, √3 ≈ 1.7. A factor of 3 applies to the energy, not the speed.

**A25.** D. used to increase the potential energy of the particles as the bonds between them are loosened
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-25 -->

Explanation: The temperature, and so the average kinetic energy, is constant during a phase change. The energy supplied raises the potential energy by loosening the arrangement of particles and so increases the internal energy.

**A26.** (a) 25 + 273 = 298 K [1]; (b) 350 − 273 = 77 °C [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-26 -->

Explanation:

**A27.** Air molecules are in constant random motion [1]; they collide with the smoke particles from all directions [1]; at any instant the collisions on different sides do not balance, so there is a net force and the smoke particle moves in a random, jerky way [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-27 -->

Explanation:

**A28.** At the same temperature the average translational kinetic energies are equal [1]; since ½mv² is equal, v is proportional to 1/√m [1]; ratio of speeds = √(16/4) = 2, so helium particles are on average twice as fast [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-28 -->

Explanation:

**A29.** When the objects are in contact, particles at the interface collide and transfer kinetic energy from the particles with greater average kinetic energy to those with less [1]; there is a net transfer from the hotter to the colder object until the average random kinetic energy per particle is the same in both [1]; at this point the temperatures are equal and there is no net energy transfer [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-29 -->

Explanation:

**A30.** Solid: particles are closely packed in fixed positions and vibrate about them [1]; liquid: particles are close together but not in fixed positions, and they move past one another [1]; gas: particles are far apart and move rapidly and randomly, with negligible forces between them except in collisions [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-30 -->

Explanation:

**A31.** (a) 120 + 273 = 393 K [1]; −153 + 273 = 120 K [1]; (b) change = 393 − 120 = 273 K (a decrease of 273 K) [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-31 -->

Explanation:

**A32.** The particles in the liquid have a range of kinetic energies, and the fastest, most energetic particles near the surface escape as vapour [1]; the particles that escape take away more than the average kinetic energy [1]; the average kinetic energy of the remaining particles therefore falls, so the temperature decreases [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-32 -->

Explanation:

**A33.** Average kinetic energy is proportional to the temperature in kelvin, not degrees Celsius [1]; 10 °C = 283 K and 20 °C = 293 K [1]; the ratio is 293/283 ≈ 1.04, so the kinetic energy increases by only about 4% and the claim is incorrect [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-33 -->

Explanation:

**A34.** B. Doubles
<!-- id: vce-physics12-tk-c1 -->

Explanation: Average kinetic energy is proportional to absolute temperature: 300 K → 600 K is a doubling. Using °C gives the wrong ratio.

#### Specific heat capacity

**A35.** A. 8.36 × 10⁴ J
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-1 -->

Explanation: Q = mcΔT = 0.500 × 4180 × 40 = 83 600 J = 8.36 × 10⁴ J. Using the final temperature (60) instead of ΔT gives 1.25 × 10⁵ J.

**A36.** C. Y undergoes the greater temperature rise
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-2 -->

Explanation: ΔT = Q/(mc). With Q and m equal, the metal with the smaller c (Y) has the greater temperature rise.

**A37.** A. 2100 J is needed to raise the temperature of 1 kg of ice by 1 K
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-3 -->

Explanation: Specific heat capacity is the energy needed to raise 1 kg of a substance by 1 K. Melting involves latent heat, not specific heat capacity.

**A38.** D. 18 K
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-4 -->

Explanation: Q = Pt = 1500 × 100 = 1.5 × 10⁵ J. ΔT = Q/(mc) = 150 000/(2.0 × 4180) = 17.9 K ≈ 18 K. Forgetting the mass gives 36 K.

**A39.** A. has a high specific heat capacity, so its temperature changes little for a large energy change
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-5 -->

Explanation: Water's high specific heat capacity means large amounts of energy are absorbed or released for small temperature changes, moderating the temperature of nearby land.

**A40.** Energy lost by Al = energy gained by water; 0.250 × 900 × (100.0 − T) = 0.400 × 4180 × (T − 20.0); 225(100 − T) = 1672(T − 20); 22 500 + 33 440 = 1897T so 55 940 = 1897T; T = 29.5 °C.
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A41.** (a) Q = mcΔT = 1.50 × 4180 × 82.0 = 5.14 × 10⁵ J; (b) t = Q/P = 514 140/2000 = 257 s; (c) energy input = 514 140/0.85 = 6.05 × 10⁵ J; t = 604 871/2000 = 302 s.
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A42.** (a) Q = Pt = 50 × 300 = 15 000 J; c = Q/(mΔT) = 15 000/(1.0 × 17) = 882 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹ (≈ 880); (b) some energy is lost to the surroundings so the block gains less energy than Pt, yet the student used the full Pt, giving too large a value; improvement: insulate the block (e.g. lag it with foam).
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A43.** B. J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-9 -->

Explanation: Specific heat capacity is energy per unit mass per unit temperature change, so its unit is J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹. J K⁻¹ is the unit of heat capacity of a whole object, which does not include the mass.

**A44.** C. 3.85 × 10⁴ J
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-10 -->

Explanation: Q = mcΔT = 2.0 × 385 × 50 = 38 500 J = 3.85 × 10⁴ J. Leaving out the mass gives 1.93 × 10⁴ J.

**A45.** D. 30 K
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-11 -->

Explanation: ΔT = Q/(mc) = 30 000/(0.50 × 2000) = 30 K. Forgetting the mass gives 15 K.

**A46.** A. mc, the mass multiplied by the specific heat capacity
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-12 -->

Explanation: Q = mcΔT, so Q plotted against ΔT is a straight line with gradient mc. The gradient 1/(mc) would apply if ΔT were plotted against Q.

**A47.** B. 40 °C
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-13 -->

Explanation: Energy lost = energy gained: 1.0 × (60 − T) = 2.0 × (T − 30), so 120 = 3T and T = 40 °C. The simple average of 45 °C ignores that there is twice as much cooler water.

**A48.** C. The water requires more energy because it has the greater specific heat capacity
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-14 -->

Explanation: Q = mcΔT, and with m and ΔT equal the energy depends on c. Water (4180) has a much higher c than copper (385), so it needs more energy.

**A49.** D. 7.7 × 10³ J
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-15 -->

Explanation: Q = mcΔT = 0.20 × 385 × 100 = 7700 J. Using the final temperature gives 3.85 × 10³ J, and using the initial temperature gives 1.16 × 10⁴ J; neither is the temperature change.

**A50.** A. can absorb a large amount of energy for each kelvin of temperature rise
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-16 -->

Explanation: Water's high specific heat capacity lets it carry away a lot of energy per kilogram with only a small temperature rise. A low specific heat capacity would make it a poor coolant.

**A51.** B. P has a lower specific heat capacity than Q
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-17 -->

Explanation: With the same energy supplied each second and equal masses, ΔT = Q/(mc) is larger for the substance with the smaller c. A higher c would give a slower rise.

**A52.** D. 450 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-18 -->

Explanation: c = Q/(mΔT) = 9000/(0.50 × 40) = 450 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹. Leaving out the mass gives 225 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹.

**A53.** A. make the temperature uniform so the thermometer reading represents the whole liquid
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-19 -->

Explanation: Stirring spreads the energy so the whole liquid is at the same temperature as the thermometer. It does not change c, and the time is still needed to find Q = Pt.

**A54.** B. closer to 20 °C than to 100 °C, because water has the greater specific heat capacity
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-20 -->

Explanation: Equal masses but c(water) is about 4.6 times c(aluminium), so the water's temperature changes less. The average of 60 °C would apply only if the specific heat capacities were equal.

**A55.** D. has a lower specific heat capacity than water, so the same energy produces a larger temperature rise
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-21 -->

Explanation: For similar absorbed energy per kilogram, ΔT = Q/(mc) is larger for sand because its c is much smaller than that of water. A higher c would make the sand cooler, not hotter.

**A56.** A. 2 : 1
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-22 -->

Explanation: With c and Q equal, ΔT = Q/(mc) is inversely proportional to mass, so the lighter sample A warms twice as much. 1 : 2 reverses the ratio.

**A57.** B. 31 K
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-23 -->

Explanation: Useful energy = 0.90 × 2400 × 60 = 1.296 × 10⁵ J, so ΔT = 129 600/4180 = 31 K. Assuming 100% efficiency gives 34 K.

**A58.** C. 35 K
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-24 -->

Explanation: A change of 1 °C equals a change of 1 K, so ΔT is the same number in either unit. Adding 273 applies to converting temperatures, not temperature changes.

**A59.** D. 8.36 × 10³ J
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-25 -->

Explanation: Q = mcΔT = 200 × 4.18 × 10 = 8360 J. Using 200 g with 4180 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹ without converting to kg gives 8.36 × 10⁶ J.

**A60.** Specific heat capacity is the energy required to raise the temperature of 1 kg of a substance by 1 K [1]; its SI unit is J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-26 -->

Explanation:

**A61.** Q = Pt = 2400 × 240 = 5.76 × 10⁵ J [1]; ΔT = Q/(mc) = 576 000/(5.0 × 4180) = 27.6 K [1]; final temperature = 15 + 27.6 = 42.6 °C [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-27 -->

Explanation:

**A62.** Measure the mass of the block with a balance [1]; heat it with an electric heater of known power (or measured voltage and current) for a measured time, recording the initial and final temperatures with a thermometer, with the block insulated to reduce losses [1]; calculate the energy supplied using Q = Pt (or VIt) and the temperature rise ΔT [1]; calculate c = Q/(mΔT) [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-28 -->

Explanation:

**A63.** Energy gained by water = 0.200 × 4180 × 6.0 = 5016 J [1]; energy lost by metal = 0.300 × c × 74.0 [1]; equate: 0.300 × c × 74.0 = 5016 [1]; c = 5016/22.2 = 226 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-29 -->

Explanation:

**A64.** Q = mcΔT = 0.80 × 900 × 45 = 3.24 × 10⁴ J [1]; t = Q/P [1] = 32 400/500 = 64.8 s ≈ 65 s [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-30 -->

Explanation:

**A65.** B. 44 °C
<!-- id: vce-physics12-sh-c1 -->

Explanation: Heat lost = heat gained: 0.200 × c × (80 − T) = 0.300 × c × (T − 20), so T = (200 × 80 + 300 × 20) ÷ 500 = 44 °C. It is closer to 20 °C because there is more cold water.

#### Latent heat and phase change

**A66.** A. 6.68 × 10⁴ J
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-1 -->

Explanation: Q = mL = 0.200 × 3.34 × 10⁵ = 6.68 × 10⁴ J. Using the latent heat of vaporisation would give 4.52 × 10⁵ J.

**A67.** C. increase the potential energy of the particles as the bonds between them are overcome
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-2 -->

Explanation: During a phase change, temperature (average KE) is constant; the energy increases the potential energy of the particles as intermolecular bonds are broken.

**A68.** A. steam releases a large amount of latent heat as it condenses on the skin
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-3 -->

Explanation: Condensation releases 2.26 × 10⁶ J kg⁻¹ before the condensed water cools further, so much more energy is transferred to the skin.

**A69.** B. boiling the water to steam
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-4 -->

Explanation: The energies are about 2.1 × 10⁴ J, 3.34 × 10⁵ J, 4.18 × 10⁵ J and 2.26 × 10⁶ J respectively. At a constant power the largest energy takes the longest time: vaporisation.

**A70.** C. 4.18 × 10⁴ J
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-5 -->

Explanation: Melting: 0.100 × 3.34 × 10⁵ = 33 400 J. Warming: 0.100 × 4180 × 20 = 8360 J. Total = 41 760 J ≈ 4.18 × 10⁴ J.

**A71.** Warm ice: 0.300 × 2100 × 10.0 = 6300 J; melt: 0.300 × 3.34 × 10⁵ = 100 200 J; warm water: 0.300 × 4180 × 100 = 125 400 J; vaporise: 0.300 × 2.26 × 10⁶ = 678 000 J; total = 909 900 J ≈ 9.10 × 10⁵ J.
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-6 -->

Explanation:

**A72.** Energy to melt ice = 0.0500 × 3.34 × 10⁵ = 16 700 J; melted ice then warms: 0.0500 × 4180 × T = 209T; warm water cools: 0.300 × 4180 × (25.0 − T) = 1254(25.0 − T); 16 700 + 209T = 31 350 − 1254T; 1463T = 14 650; T = 10.0 °C.
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-7 -->

Explanation:

**A73.** Temperature depends on average kinetic energy of the particles; during melting the supplied energy is used to break the bonds holding particles in a fixed lattice, increasing their potential energy; the kinetic energy (and so temperature) does not change until all the ice has melted.
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-8 -->

Explanation:

**A74.** A. The energy needed to melt 1 kg of the solid at its melting point without a change in temperature
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-9 -->

Explanation: It is the energy per kilogram to change solid to liquid at constant temperature. Raising the temperature by 1 K describes specific heat capacity, not latent heat.

**A75.** B. J kg⁻¹
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-10 -->

Explanation: Q = mL, so L = Q/m has units J kg⁻¹. The unit J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹ belongs to specific heat capacity, which involves a temperature change.

**A76.** C. 1.13 × 10⁶ J
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-11 -->

Explanation: Q = mL = 0.500 × 2.26 × 10⁶ = 1.13 × 10⁶ J. Forgetting to multiply by the mass gives 2.26 × 10⁶ J.

**A77.** D. 0.500 kg
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-12 -->

Explanation: m = Q/L = 1.67 × 10⁵ ÷ 3.34 × 10⁵ = 0.500 kg. Inverting the ratio gives 2.00 kg.

**A78.** A. changes of state, where energy goes into potential energy and temperature is constant
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-13 -->

Explanation: Flat sections are phase changes: energy is absorbed but used to change particle separation (potential energy), so the temperature does not change. Energy is still being absorbed.

**A79.** B. vaporisation completely separates the particles and also does work pushing back the atmosphere
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-14 -->

Explanation: Turning liquid into gas removes almost all intermolecular attraction and requires expansion against the atmosphere, whereas melting only loosens the bonds. Higher temperature is not the reason, because the average kinetic energy does not change during the phase change.

**A80.** C. 8.35 × 10⁴ J
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-15 -->

Explanation: Q = mL = 0.250 × 3.34 × 10⁵ = 8.35 × 10⁴ J released. Using 4180 × 100 would wrongly include cooling to 0 °C, which has already happened.

**A81.** D. the most energetic particles escape the liquid, lowering the average kinetic energy of those left
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-16 -->

Explanation: Only the fastest particles have enough energy to escape, so the average kinetic energy (and temperature) of the remaining liquid falls. Escaping particles do not become colder by gaining energy; they carry energy away.

**A82.** A. 66.8 s
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-17 -->

Explanation: Energy = 0.100 × 3.34 × 10⁵ = 3.34 × 10⁴ J. Time = E/P = 33 400 ÷ 500 = 66.8 s. Using the mass 1 kg would give 668 s.

**A83.** B. 4.52 × 10⁴ J
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-18 -->

Explanation: Q = mL = 0.0200 × 2.26 × 10⁶ = 4.52 × 10⁴ J. The question stops at water at 100 °C, so no cooling energy should be added.

**A84.** C. decreases because the potential energy of the particles decreases
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-19 -->

Explanation: Freezing releases latent heat, so internal energy falls. Since the temperature is constant the kinetic energy is unchanged; it is the potential energy that decreases as bonds form.

**A85.** D. a lower specific heat capacity than the liquid
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-20 -->

Explanation: With constant power, ΔT/time = P/(mc). A steeper slope means a smaller c. Mass is the same in both sections, and latent heat only affects the flat sections.

**A86.** B. changes directly from solid to gas without becoming liquid
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-21 -->

Explanation: Sublimation is solid to gas directly, as for dry ice (solid CO₂) at atmospheric pressure. Gas to liquid is condensation.

**A87.** C. absorbs a large amount of latent heat as it melts
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-22 -->

Explanation: Melting absorbs 3.34 × 10⁵ J per kg from the drink with no temperature rise, in addition to the energy absorbed warming the melt water. Both samples are at 0 °C and the same mass has the same number of particles.

**A88.** A. 0.0500 kg
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-23 -->

Explanation: m = Q/L = 1.13 × 10⁵ ÷ 2.26 × 10⁶ = 0.0500 kg. Using the latent heat of fusion would give 0.338 kg.

**A89.** B. 2.47 × 10⁵ J
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-24 -->

Explanation: Condensing: 0.100 × 2.26 × 10⁶ = 226 000 J. Cooling: 0.100 × 4180 × 50.0 = 20 900 J. Total = 246 900 J ≈ 2.47 × 10⁵ J. Counting only condensation gives 2.26 × 10⁵ J.

**A90.** Specific latent heat of vaporisation is the energy required to change 1 kg of a liquid to a gas at its boiling point without a change in temperature [1]; unit J kg⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-25 -->

Explanation:

**A91.** Energy released by condensing = 0.0400 × 2.26 × 10⁶ = 90 400 J [1]; energy released by condensed water cooling = 0.0400 × 4180 × (100 − T) [1]; energy gained by cool water = 0.500 × 4180 × (T − 20.0) [1]; equate: 90 400 + 167.2(100 − T) = 2090(T − 20.0), giving T = 66.0 °C [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-26 -->

Explanation:

**A92.** Temperature rises steadily as ice warms to 0 °C [1]; a horizontal section at 0 °C while the ice melts [1]; a rise to 100 °C that is less steep than the ice section, as water has a higher specific heat capacity [1]; a longer horizontal section at 100 °C during boiling because L vaporisation is much greater than L fusion, then a steady rise as steam is heated [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-27 -->

Explanation:

**A93.** Energy released by water cooling = 0.250 × 4180 × 30.0 = 31 350 J [1]; energy absorbed by melting ice = mL, so 31 350 = m × 3.34 × 10⁵ [1]; m = 0.0939 kg [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-28 -->

Explanation:

**A94.** Energy to cool water = 0.400 × 4180 × 20.0 = 33 440 J; energy to freeze = 0.400 × 3.34 × 10⁵ = 133 600 J; total = 167 040 J [1]; t = E/P [1]; t = 167 040 ÷ 150 = 1.11 × 10³ s (about 18.6 minutes) [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-29 -->

Explanation:

**A95.** Energy to warm ice to 0 °C = 0.100 × 2100 × 20.0 = 4200 J; energy to melt = 0.100 × 3.34 × 10⁵ = 33 400 J; total 37 600 J, less than the 66 880 J available from cooling the water to 0 °C so all the ice melts [1]; energy to warm melted ice: 0.100 × 4180 × T = 418T [1]; energy released by warm water: 0.400 × 4180 × (40.0 − T) = 1672(40.0 − T) [1]; 37 600 + 418T = 66 880 − 1672T, giving T = 14.0 °C [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-30 -->

Explanation:

**A96.** About 2.2 × 10⁵ J
<!-- id: vce-physics12-lh-c1 -->

Explanation: Warm the ice: 0.50 × 2100 × 10 = 10 500 J. Melt it: 0.50 × 3.34 × 10⁵ = 167 000 J. Warm the water: 0.50 × 4200 × 20 = 42 000 J. Total ≈ 219 500 J. Melting needs by far the most energy.

#### Conduction, convection and radiation

**A97.** B. radiation
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-1 -->

Explanation: Radiation is transferred by electromagnetic waves and requires no medium. Conduction and convection need particles.

**A98.** C. they contain delocalised electrons that transfer energy quickly
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-2 -->

Explanation: Free (delocalised) electrons move rapidly through the metal and transfer kinetic energy, in addition to lattice vibrations being passed on.

**A99.** A. warm air over the land rising and cooler, denser air from the sea moving in to replace it
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-3 -->

Explanation: Land heats faster than water, warming the air above it. This air expands, becomes less dense and rises (convection), and cooler air from the sea flows in.

**A100.** D. 20 W
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-4 -->

Explanation: Conduction rate is inversely proportional to thickness for a given area and temperature difference. Doubling the thickness halves the rate: 20 W.

**A101.** B. the trapped layer of air or vacuum between the panes is a poor conductor and limits convection
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-5 -->

Explanation: A thin trapped air (or evacuated) layer has low thermal conductivity and is too thin for significant convection currents, so conduction and convection losses are reduced.

**A102.** The tile is a better thermal conductor than the carpet; energy is conducted from the warmer foot to the tile at a faster rate; skin temperature drops more quickly (and the carpet, a poor conductor, stays warm at the surface), so the tile feels colder even though both are at the same temperature.
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A103.** Energy is conducted from the element through the base of the pot to the water at the bottom; the heated water expands, becomes less dense and rises while cooler, denser water sinks, forming convection currents; this circulation transports energy throughout the water to the top.
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A104.** Evacuated space between the walls reduces conduction and convection (no particles to transfer energy); silvered surfaces reduce radiation (low emissivity, reflecting infrared); insulating stopper reduces conduction and convection at the opening (poor conductor and traps air).
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-8 -->

Explanation:

**A105.** C. fluids (liquids and gases)
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-9 -->

Explanation: Convection relies on bulk movement of a fluid, which can flow and form currents. Solids cannot circulate in this way, and a vacuum has no particles to move.

**A106.** A. vibrations passed between neighbouring particles
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-10 -->

Explanation: Non-metals have no free electrons, so energy passes from particle to particle as vibrations. Delocalised electrons are the extra mechanism in metals only.

**A107.** D. matt black
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-11 -->

Explanation: Dark, matt surfaces absorb most of the radiation that falls on them. Light, shiny surfaces reflect much of it.

**A108.** B. 60 W
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-12 -->

Explanation: Rate of conduction is directly proportional to area, so doubling the area doubles the rate to 60 W. It is the thickness, not the area, that has an inverse relationship.

**A109.** C. 50 W
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-13 -->

Explanation: Rate of conduction is directly proportional to temperature difference, so doubling it doubles the rate to 50 W. Squaring the change (100 W) would be wrong because the relationship is linear.

**A110.** A. 800 W
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-14 -->

Explanation: P = 0.80 × 2.0 × 5.0 ÷ 0.010 = 800 W. Multiplying by the thickness instead of dividing gives the incorrect 0.080 W.

**A111.** D. expands and becomes less dense than the surrounding air
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-15 -->

Explanation: Heated air expands, so its density falls below that of the cooler air outside and it is pushed upwards. Heating does not increase the mass of air in the balloon.

**A112.** B. heated water at the base rises while cooler water sinks, setting up convection currents
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-16 -->

Explanation: Water at the base expands, becomes less dense and rises, while denser cool water sinks to replace it. Hot water does not sink, which makes the first option wrong.

**A113.** C. cool air from the unit is denser and sinks, so warmer air rises and is drawn in to be cooled
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-17 -->

Explanation: Cooled air is denser and sinks, pushing warmer air up towards the unit and creating a convection current. Cool air does not rise, so the first option has the density relationship reversed.

**A114.** A. W m⁻¹ K⁻¹
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-18 -->

Explanation: From P = kAΔT/L, k = PL/(AΔT), which has units W m⁻¹ K⁻¹. J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹ is the unit of specific heat capacity.

**A115.** D. hot air rising from a bitumen road on a summer day
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-19 -->

Explanation: Air heated by the road expands and rises, carrying energy upwards as a convection current. Warmth from the Sun or a lamp is mainly radiation, and the spoon warms by conduction.

**A116.** B. they trap many small pockets of air, which conducts poorly and cannot circulate easily
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-20 -->

Explanation: The fibres hold air in tiny pockets that limit both conduction (air is a poor conductor) and convection. No material is a perfect insulator.

**A117.** C. It travels through space as electromagnetic waves at the speed of light
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-21 -->

Explanation: Thermal radiation is electromagnetic radiation, so it needs no medium and moves at 3.00 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹ in a vacuum. Space has no particles to support convection.

**A118.** A. absorbed at the same rate as it is emitted
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-22 -->

Explanation: All objects above absolute zero both emit and absorb radiation continuously. A steady temperature means the two rates are equal, not that either has stopped.

**A119.** D. increases
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-23 -->

Explanation: Hotter objects emit radiation at a greater rate regardless of surface type. A shiny surface lowers the amount emitted at any temperature but does not reverse the trend.

**A120.** B. metal has a higher thermal conductivity
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-24 -->

Explanation: Metal conducts energy far more quickly than wood, so energy reaches the far end sooner. Both rods start at the same temperature, so the difference is not due to initial temperature.

**A121.** A. reflect infrared radiation from the body back towards the person
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-25 -->

Explanation: The reflective surface has low emissivity and sends the body's radiated energy back, reducing loss. Absorbing it would simply warm the blanket's outer surface and lose energy to the air.

**A122.** C. 48 W
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-26 -->

Explanation: Rate is proportional to area and inversely proportional to length, so the factor is 2 × 2 = 4, giving 48 W. Applying only one of the changes gives 24 W.

**A123.** B. the land cools faster than the sea, so warmer air over the sea rises and cooler air from the land replaces it
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-27 -->

Explanation: Water cools more slowly than land, so air over the sea stays warmer and rises, and cooler air flows in from the land. The reverse of the daytime sea breeze occurs at night.

**A124.** Air next to the radiator is heated, expands and becomes less dense, so it rises [1]; cooler, denser air moves in to replace it, producing a convection current that circulates warm air around the room [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-28 -->

Explanation:

**A125.** Substitute into P = kAΔT/L [1]; P = 0.040 × 12 × 15 ÷ 0.10 [1]; P = 72 W [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-29 -->

Explanation:

**A126.** The matt black surface is a good absorber of radiation, so it absorbs most of the radiation from the Sun [1]; the white surface reflects much of the incident radiation, so less energy is absorbed and the white car warms less [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-30 -->

Explanation:

**A127.** The feathers trap many small pockets of air [1]; air is a poor conductor, so conduction of energy away from the body is reduced [1]; the pockets are too small for convection currents to form, so the trapped air stays still and convection loss is also reduced [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-31 -->

Explanation:

**A128.** Conduction transfers energy through a material by collisions or vibrations between neighbouring particles, with no overall movement of the material [1]; convection transfers energy by the bulk movement of a fluid, with heated, less dense fluid rising and cooler fluid sinking [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-32 -->

Explanation:

**A129.** Convert thickness to metres: L = 4.0 × 10⁻³ m [1]; P = 0.84 × 1.5 × 6.0 ÷ (4.0 × 10⁻³) [1]; P = 1.9 × 10³ W (1890 W) [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-33 -->

Explanation:

**A130.** Convection currents carry heated air upwards, away from a person standing to the side, and air is a poor conductor [1]; the fire emits infrared radiation in all directions, which travels through the air and is absorbed by the person's skin [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-34 -->

Explanation:

**A131.** A large surface area increases the area in contact with the air, so more energy is transferred by conduction to the air and convection [1]; matt black surfaces are good emitters of radiation, so more energy is radiated away [1]; warmed air near the fins rises and is replaced by cooler air, which maintains a convection current [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-35 -->

Explanation:

**A132.** Metal is a good thermal conductor, so energy is rapidly conducted along the spoon (by free electrons and vibrations) to the handle [1]; wood is a poor conductor, so energy travels along it very slowly and the handle stays cool [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-ccr-36 -->

Explanation:

**A133.** A. The vacuum stops conduction and convection, and the silvered walls reduce radiation
<!-- id: vce-physics12-cc-c1 -->

Explanation: Conduction and convection need particles; radiation does not, so shiny surfaces are used to reflect it back.

#### Thermal radiation and Wien's law

**A134.** B. 500 nm
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-1 -->

Explanation: λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 5800 = 5.0 × 10⁻⁷ m = 500 nm, in the visible region.

**A135.** D. The peak wavelength decreases and total emitted power increases
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-2 -->

Explanation: Wien's law: λmax is inversely proportional to T, so peak wavelength shifts to shorter values. Hotter objects also emit more power overall.

**A136.** C. infrared region
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-3 -->

Explanation: λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 310 = 9.35 × 10⁻⁶ m, about 9.4 µm, which is infrared.

**A137.** C. twice as great
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-4 -->

Explanation: λmax is inversely proportional to T. A has half the peak wavelength, so twice the surface temperature.

**A138.** A. The matte black cup radiates more energy per second
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-5 -->

Explanation: Good absorbers are good emitters. A matte black surface has high emissivity and radiates more strongly than shiny silver at the same temperature.

**A139.** (a) T = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 1.45 × 10⁻⁶ = 2.00 × 10³ K; (b) peak lies in the infrared; most energy emitted is infrared, not visible, so the lamp is inefficient as a light source.
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-6 -->

Explanation:

**A140.** λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 288 (1); = 1.01 × 10⁻⁵ m, or about 10 µm (1); this is in the infrared region (1).
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-7 -->

Explanation:

**A141.** The graph is a continuous curve with a single peak; as temperature increases the peak moves to a shorter wavelength; the intensity is greater at all wavelengths so the area under the curve (total power emitted) increases.
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-8 -->

Explanation:

**A142.** A. The product of the peak wavelength and the absolute temperature is constant
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-9 -->

Explanation: Wien's law is λmax × T = 2.898 × 10⁻³ m K, so λmax is inversely proportional to T. A direct proportionality would mean hotter objects peak at longer wavelengths, which is the opposite of what is observed.

**A143.** B. 970 nm
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-10 -->

Explanation: λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 3000 = 9.66 × 10⁻⁷ m, about 970 nm. Options in µm come from dividing incorrectly by a power of ten.

**A144.** C. 290 K
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-11 -->

Explanation: T = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 1.0 × 10⁻⁵ = 290 K. Forgetting to convert 10 µm to metres gives an answer out by a power of ten.

**A145.** D. halve
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-12 -->

Explanation: The temperatures are 300 K and 600 K, so the absolute temperature doubles and the peak wavelength halves. The temperature in °C changes by a factor of 12, but Wien's law must use kelvin.

**A146.** A. The blue star is hotter because its peak wavelength is shorter
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-13 -->

Explanation: Blue light has a shorter wavelength than red light, and by Wien's law a shorter peak wavelength means a higher temperature. A longer peak wavelength indicates a cooler star.

**A147.** B. It is emitted by all objects with a temperature above absolute zero
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-14 -->

Explanation: All matter above 0 K emits electromagnetic radiation because of the thermal motion of its charged particles. Most objects at everyday temperatures emit in the infrared, so they do not glow visibly.

**A148.** C. electromagnetic radiation
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-15 -->

Explanation: Radiation is the only form of heat transfer that needs no medium. Conduction and convection both need particles, which are absent in the vacuum of space.

**A149.** D. absorbs all incident radiation and emits the maximum possible thermal radiation for its temperature
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-16 -->

Explanation: A blackbody is a perfect absorber and therefore also a perfect emitter. It is not necessarily black in colour: the Sun is a close approximation yet appears yellow-white.

**A150.** A. reflects more of the incident radiation and absorbs less
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-17 -->

Explanation: Light, shiny surfaces reflect more incident radiation, so less energy is absorbed. A black shirt absorbs more of the Sun's radiation and warms up faster.

**A151.** B. is a poor emitter and a good reflector of thermal radiation
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-18 -->

Explanation: A silvered surface has low emissivity and reflects radiation back, reducing transfer by radiation. The vacuum between the walls, not the silvering, is what stops conduction and convection.

**A152.** C. The cup emits and absorbs radiation at the same rate
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-19 -->

Explanation: In thermal equilibrium the object still emits radiation, but it absorbs radiation from its surroundings at the same rate, so there is no net energy transfer and its temperature stays constant.

**A153.** D. 1.0 × 10⁴ K
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-20 -->

Explanation: T = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 2.90 × 10⁻⁷ = 1.0 × 10⁴ K. A 290 nm peak is in the ultraviolet, which indicates a very hot object.

**A154.** A. 3
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-21 -->

Explanation: Since λmax is proportional to 1/T, the ratio λX/λY = TY/TX = 900/300 = 3. Inverting this ratio is a common mistake.

**A155.** B. infrared region
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-22 -->

Explanation: λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 1000 = 2.9 µm, which is infrared. Only the short-wavelength tail of the spectrum reaches the red end of the visible region, which is why the element glows dull red.

**A156.** C. 1.1 mm
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-23 -->

Explanation: λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 2.7 = 1.07 × 10⁻³ m, about 1.1 mm, which is in the microwave region. Very low temperatures give very long peak wavelengths.

**A157.** D. is a good absorber of radiation
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-24 -->

Explanation: A matte black surface absorbs a large fraction of the incident radiation, so more of the Sun's energy heats the water. A reflective surface would send much of it away.

**A158.** A. 7.2 µm
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-25 -->

Explanation: T = 127 + 273 = 400 K, so λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 400 = 7.2 × 10⁻⁶ m. Using 127 instead of 400 gives the incorrect 23 µm.

**A159.** C. total power emitted per unit area
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-26 -->

Explanation: Adding the intensity over all wavelengths gives the total power emitted per unit area. The peak position only tells you the wavelength of greatest intensity, not the total.

**A160.** T = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 4.50 × 10⁻⁷ [1]; T = 6.44 × 10³ K [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-27 -->

Explanation:

**A161.** The peak wavelength halves because it is inversely proportional to the absolute temperature [1]; the peak moves towards the visible region so a greater proportion of the emitted radiation is visible and the sphere glows brighter [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-28 -->

Explanation:

**A162.** Furnace: λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 1500 = 1.93 × 10⁻⁶ m [1]; bench: λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 300 = 9.66 × 10⁻⁶ m [1]; the hotter furnace has a peak wavelength five times shorter than the bench [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-29 -->

Explanation:

**A163.** Conduction and convection require a medium (particles) to carry the energy, and space between the Sun and Earth is almost a vacuum [1]; radiation is electromagnetic waves, which can travel through a vacuum without a medium [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-30 -->

Explanation:

**A164.** A dark surface is a good absorber so it absorbs more of the Sun's incident radiation than the white surface [1]; good absorbers are also good emitters, so the dark surface has a higher emissivity [1]; therefore at night the dark car radiates more energy per second and cools faster [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-31 -->

Explanation:

**A165.** The vacuum between the walls prevents conduction and convection across the gap [1]; the silvered surfaces are poor emitters of thermal radiation [1]; they also reflect radiation back towards the hot contents, so little energy is lost by radiation [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-32 -->

Explanation:

**A166.** Betelgeuse: λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 3500 = 8.28 × 10⁻⁷ m [1]; Rigel: λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 12 000 = 2.42 × 10⁻⁷ m [1]; Betelgeuse's peak is in the infrared with its visible output strongest at the red end, so it appears red [1]; Rigel's peak is in the ultraviolet with strong output at the blue end of the visible, so it appears blue-white [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-33 -->

Explanation:

**A167.** (a) T = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 1.2 × 10⁻⁶ = 2.42 × 10³ K [1]; (b) new temperature = 3.42 × 10³ K [1]; λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 3415 = 8.5 × 10⁻⁷ m [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-34 -->

Explanation:

**A168.** T = 100 + 273 = 373 K [1]; λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 373 = 7.77 × 10⁻⁶ m [1]; this is in the infrared region [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-35 -->

Explanation:

**A169.** A blackbody is an ideal object that absorbs all radiation incident on it and emits the maximum possible thermal radiation at its temperature [1]; radiation entering the small hole is reflected many times inside the cavity and is almost completely absorbed, so almost none escapes [1]; the radiation leaving the hole has a spectrum that depends only on the cavity temperature [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-36 -->

Explanation:

**A170.** T = 80 + 273 = 353 K [1]; λmax = 2.898 × 10⁻³ / 353 = 8.21 × 10⁻⁶ m, in the infrared [1]; the hotter tea emits more infrared radiation per second than its surroundings [1]; the camera detects this greater intensity so the cup appears brighter [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wien-37 -->

Explanation:

**A171.** A. 5800 K
<!-- id: vce-physics12-tw-c1 -->

Explanation: T = 2.9 × 10⁻³ ÷ 500 × 10⁻⁹ = 5800 K. By contrast, Earth (about 288 K) peaks near 10 µm, in the infrared.

#### Climate and the enhanced greenhouse effect

**A172.** A. methane, CH₄
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-1 -->

Explanation: Methane absorbs infrared radiation. The main components of air (N₂, O₂, Ar) do not significantly absorb infrared.

**A173.** B. Greenhouse gases absorb infrared radiation emitted by the Earth and re-emit it in all directions, some returning to the surface
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-2 -->

Explanation: The Earth's surface re-emits absorbed sunlight as infrared. Greenhouse gases absorb this and re-emit in all directions, so part is returned to the surface, warming it.

**A174.** D. additional warming caused by increased concentrations of greenhouse gases from human activity
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-3 -->

Explanation: The enhanced greenhouse effect refers to extra warming from increased greenhouse gas concentrations, e.g. from burning fossil fuels and land clearing.

**A175.** A. more sunlight absorbed, which warms the region further (a positive feedback)
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-4 -->

Explanation: Ice is highly reflective; open ocean is dark and absorbs more radiation. This causes further warming and melting: a positive feedback.

**A176.** C. increase until the rates are equal again
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-5 -->

Explanation: A net energy gain raises the temperature. The Earth then radiates more strongly until a new equilibrium is reached.

**A177.** Natural greenhouse effect: warming from greenhouse gases that occur naturally in the atmosphere (e.g. H₂O, CO₂, CH₄); enhanced: additional warming due to increased greenhouse gas concentrations from human activity such as fossil fuel combustion; the natural effect keeps the Earth's average surface temperature at about 288 K (15 °C) rather than about 255 K (−18 °C), allowing liquid water.
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-6 -->

Explanation:

**A178.** Thermal expansion: as the oceans warm, the water expands, increasing its volume; melting of land-based ice (glaciers and ice sheets, e.g. Greenland and Antarctica) adds water to the oceans; floating sea ice contributes little because it already displaces its own mass of water (any two, with explanation).
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-7 -->

Explanation:

**A179.** Sunlight is mostly visible and passes through the atmosphere largely unabsorbed to heat the surface; the Earth, at a much lower temperature, emits at longer wavelengths (infrared, by Wien's law); greenhouse gases absorb infrared strongly, trapping energy and re-emitting some back to the surface.
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-8 -->

Explanation:

**A180.** A. water vapour, H₂O
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-9 -->

Explanation: Water vapour is the most abundant greenhouse gas and contributes the most to the natural effect. CO₂ is the most important of the gases that humans are increasing, but it is not the largest natural contributor.

**A181.** B. the vibration frequencies of carbon dioxide molecules match infrared frequencies, so they absorb it, whereas N₂ does not
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-10 -->

Explanation: CO₂ molecules have vibrational modes that absorb infrared radiation, whereas diatomic N₂ essentially does not. Nitrogen is in fact far more abundant than CO₂, so quantity is not the reason.

**A182.** C. fraction of incident radiation that is reflected by the surface
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-11 -->

Explanation: Albedo is the ratio of reflected to incident radiation, so a value near 1 means a highly reflective surface. The absorbed fraction is 1 minus the albedo, which is a common mix-up.

**A183.** D. 10 µm
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-12 -->

Explanation: λ_max = 2.898 × 10⁻³ ÷ 288 ≈ 1.0 × 10⁻⁵ m = 10 µm, which is in the infrared. Forgetting to convert metres to micrometres gives the tempting wrong values.

**A184.** A. 16
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-13 -->

Explanation: Doubling T multiplies T⁴ by 2⁴ = 16. A factor of 2 would apply only if power were directly proportional to T.

**A185.** B. raising livestock such as cattle and sheep
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-14 -->

Explanation: Ruminant livestock, rice paddies, landfill and fossil fuel extraction release methane. Cement production mainly releases CO₂, not methane.

**A186.** C. air bubbles trapped in layers of ice cores
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-15 -->

Explanation: Air trapped in bubbles in deep polar ice is a sample of the ancient atmosphere, and isotope ratios in the ice give past temperatures. Surface features of present-day ice cannot give past gas concentrations.

**A187.** D. Warmer oceans dissolve less CO₂, so more CO₂ stays in the atmosphere and causes further warming
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-16 -->

Explanation: Gases are less soluble in warmer water, so warming releases CO₂ that causes more warming, which amplifies the original change. The other options describe processes that reduce or oppose warming.

**A188.** A. amplifies the warming, acting as a positive feedback
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-17 -->

Explanation: More water vapour absorbs more infrared radiation, increasing warming and so increasing evaporation further. Being natural does not stop water vapour from absorbing infrared.

**A189.** B. more acidic, because carbonic acid forms
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-18 -->

Explanation: Dissolved CO₂ reacts with water to form carbonic acid, lowering the pH of seawater (ocean acidification). Carbon dioxide is acidic, not basic.

**A190.** C. 340 W m⁻²
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-19 -->

Explanation: The sphere's area is four times the disc's area, so the average is 1361 ÷ 4 ≈ 340 W m⁻². Dividing by 2 would wrongly assume the sphere is only twice the disc area.

**A191.** D. 238 W m⁻²
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-20 -->

Explanation: Absorbed = (1 − 0.30) × 340 = 238 W m⁻². The value 102 W m⁻² is the reflected portion (0.30 × 340), which is a common mistake.

**A192.** A. 0.10 m
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-21 -->

Explanation: Fractional change = 2.0 × 10⁻⁴ × 0.50 = 1.0 × 10⁻⁴, so the rise is 1000 × 1.0 × 10⁻⁴ = 0.10 m. Using the full 1 K instead of 0.50 K would give 0.20 m.

**A193.** B. 4.1 × 10¹⁴ W
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-22 -->

Explanation: Power = 0.80 × 5.1 × 10¹⁴ = 4.1 × 10¹⁴ W. The other values come from errors in the powers of ten or in the multiplication.

**A194.** C. It is a positive feedback because warming releases greenhouse gases that cause further warming
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-23 -->

Explanation: Warming thaws permafrost, which releases greenhouse gases and causes more warming, so the effect is amplified. A feedback is any process driven by the change that in turn affects that change, whether or not humans started it.

**A195.** D. aerosols reflect incoming sunlight back to space, increasing the albedo
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-24 -->

Explanation: Sulfate aerosols in the stratosphere reflect sunlight, so less energy reaches the surface. Eruptions do release some CO₂ but not enough to offset this short-term cooling.

**A196.** A. 1 000 000 molecules of air, about 280 are CO₂
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-25 -->

Explanation: ppm means parts per million, so 280 ppm is 280 molecules of CO₂ per million molecules of air. Confusing ppm with a percentage would greatly overestimate the amount.

**A197.** B. fresh snow
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-26 -->

Explanation: Fresh snow reflects up to about 80–90% of incident sunlight, whereas ocean, forest and dark soil reflect only a small fraction. This is why loss of snow and ice is significant for warming.

**A198.** C. Climate is the average of weather conditions over a long period, typically decades, whereas weather is the state of the atmosphere over a short period
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-27 -->

Explanation: Climate is the long-term statistical pattern of weather, usually averaged over about 30 years. A single cold day is weather and says little about the climate trend.

**A199.** Burning fossil fuels releases carbon dioxide (a greenhouse gas) into the atmosphere, increasing its concentration [1]; the extra greenhouse gas absorbs and re-emits more of the infrared radiation emitted by the Earth, returning more energy to the surface and raising the average temperature [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-28 -->

Explanation:

**A200.** λ_max = 2.898 × 10⁻³ ÷ 330 = 8.8 × 10⁻⁶ m (8.8 µm) [1]; this is in the infrared region [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-29 -->

Explanation:

**A201.** Snow compacts into ice and traps small bubbles of air from the time it fell [1]; analysing the gas in the bubbles at different depths gives past CO₂ concentrations, with deeper ice being older [1]; the ratios of isotopes (e.g. oxygen or hydrogen) in the ice depend on the temperature at the time, giving past temperatures [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-30 -->

Explanation:

**A202.** A positive feedback is a process in which an initial change causes a further change in the same direction, amplifying the original effect [1]; warming thaws permafrost, releasing trapped methane and CO₂ [1]; these greenhouse gases enhance warming, causing more thawing [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-31 -->

Explanation:

**A203.** At equilibrium, the rate at which the Earth absorbs solar energy equals the rate at which it radiates energy to space [1]; extra CO₂ absorbs more of the infrared emitted by the surface and re-emits some back downward, so less energy escapes to space [1]; absorbed energy now exceeds energy radiated, so the Earth gains energy and its temperature rises [1]; as it warms, it emits more radiation until emitted and absorbed rates are equal again at a higher temperature [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-32 -->

Explanation:

**A204.** Fractional increase in volume = 2.1 × 10⁻⁴ × 0.80 = 1.68 × 10⁻⁴ [1]; for a fixed area, the fractional change in depth equals the fractional change in volume [1]; rise = 800 × 1.68 × 10⁻⁴ = 0.13 m [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-33 -->

Explanation:

**A205.** Net power = 0.60 × 5.1 × 10¹⁴ = 3.1 × 10¹⁴ W [1]; energy = power × time [1]; E = 3.06 × 10¹⁴ × 3.15 × 10⁷ = 9.6 × 10²¹ J [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-34 -->

Explanation:

**A206.** CO₂ dissolves in seawater to form carbonic acid, lowering the pH of the oceans (ocean acidification) [1]; this makes it harder for organisms such as corals and shellfish to build calcium carbonate shells and skeletons, threatening marine ecosystems [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-35 -->

Explanation:

**A207.** A large eruption injects sulfate aerosols into the upper atmosphere that reflect incoming sunlight, increasing albedo and cooling the Earth [1]; this effect is short lived (a year or two) as the aerosols settle out [1]; increased CO₂ absorbs infrared emitted by the Earth, trapping more energy and causing long-lasting warming [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-36 -->

Explanation:

**A208.** A cold week is a short-term weather event, the state of the atmosphere over a short time [1]; climate is the average of weather over long periods (decades), so global warming is assessed from long-term trends in average temperature [1]; a single cold week is consistent with warming because short-term variation occurs about a rising average, so the claim is not valid [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-gh-37 -->

Explanation:

**A209.** D. Absorb infrared radiation emitted by the Earth's surface and re-emit some of it back towards the surface
<!-- id: vce-physics12-cg-c1 -->

Explanation: Sunlight (mostly visible) passes through; the warmed surface emits infrared, which CO₂, methane and water vapour absorb. More of these gases enhances the effect.

### Unit 1: Light

#### Wave model of light

**A210.** A. 5.00 × 10⁻⁷ m
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-1 -->

Explanation: λ = c/f = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 6.00 × 10¹⁴ = 5.00 × 10⁻⁷ m (500 nm).

**A211.** C. the slit width is comparable to the wavelength
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-2 -->

Explanation: Diffraction is significant when the opening is of the same order of magnitude as the wavelength.

**A212.** A. a transverse wave
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-3 -->

Explanation: Only transverse waves, which oscillate perpendicular to the direction of travel, can be polarised.

**A213.** C. speed decreases, wavelength decreases, frequency unchanged
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-4 -->

Explanation: Frequency is set by the source and does not change. Light slows in glass, so v = fλ shows the wavelength decreases.

**A214.** B. destructive, giving a dark region
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-5 -->

Explanation: A path difference of (n + ½)λ gives waves exactly out of phase, so destructive interference. A whole number of wavelengths would give constructive interference.

**A215.** (a) f = c/λ = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 650 × 10⁻⁹ = 4.62 × 10¹⁴ Hz; (b) v = c/n = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 1.50 = 2.00 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹; (c) λ = v/f = 2.00 × 10⁸ / 4.62 × 10¹⁴ = 4.33 × 10⁻⁷ m (433 nm).
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-6 -->

Explanation:

**A216.** Intensity is a maximum when axes are parallel and decreases to (almost) zero when the filters are at 90° (crossed); this occurs because only the component of the light's oscillation along the transmission axis passes through; sound is a longitudinal wave, whose oscillations are along the direction of travel, so there is no transverse direction to filter.
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-7 -->

Explanation:

**A217.** Diffraction is significant when the opening is comparable to the wavelength; visible light has a wavelength of about 5 × 10⁻⁷ m, far smaller than a doorway (about 1 m), so there is negligible spreading; audible sound has wavelengths of about 0.1 m to several metres, comparable to the doorway, so it diffracts noticeably.
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-8 -->

Explanation:

**A218.** A. It consists of oscillating electric and magnetic fields perpendicular to each other and to the direction of travel
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-9 -->

Explanation: Light is a transverse electromagnetic wave with electric and magnetic fields at right angles to each other and to the direction of travel. Unlike sound, it needs no medium.

**A219.** B. Microwaves
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-10 -->

Explanation: Of these, microwaves have the longest wavelength (about 10⁻³ m to 10⁻¹ m). X-rays and ultraviolet are shorter than visible light.

**A220.** C. 4.29 × 10¹⁴ Hz
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-11 -->

Explanation: f = c/λ = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 7.00 × 10⁻⁷ = 4.29 × 10¹⁴ Hz. The 10¹⁷ option comes from forgetting to convert nanometres correctly.

**A221.** D. 1.24 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-12 -->

Explanation: v = c/n = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 2.42 = 1.24 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹. Multiplying by n would give a speed greater than c, which is impossible.

**A222.** A. a pattern of alternating bright and dark fringes caused by interference
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-13 -->

Explanation: Alternating bright and dark fringes can only be explained by interference of waves. A particle model predicts just two bright bands behind the slits.

**A223.** B. increase
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-14 -->

Explanation: Fringe spacing Δx = λL/d, so decreasing the slit separation d increases Δx. Larger spacing is the opposite of the decrease tempting from 'closer together'.

**A224.** C. decrease, because blue light has a shorter wavelength
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-15 -->

Explanation: Δx = λL/d and blue light has a shorter wavelength than red, so the fringes are closer together. Higher frequency does not increase spacing.

**A225.** D. 4.5 × 10⁻³ m
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-16 -->

Explanation: Δx = λL/d = (6.00 × 10⁻⁷ × 1.5) / (2.0 × 10⁻⁴) = 4.5 × 10⁻³ m. Forgetting to convert 0.20 mm to metres gives 4.5 × 10⁻⁶ m.

**A226.** A. a crest from one slit arrives together with a trough from the other slit
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-17 -->

Explanation: Dark fringes are regions of destructive interference, where waves arrive out of phase. Light does reach that point from both slits; it cancels there.

**A227.** B. A central bright maximum that is wider and brighter than the other maxima
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-18 -->

Explanation: The central maximum is the brightest and about twice as wide as the others. Equal fringes are characteristic of a double slit, not a single slit.

**A228.** C. becomes wider
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-19 -->

Explanation: Diffraction is more pronounced as the slit width approaches the wavelength, so the light spreads more and the central maximum widens.

**A229.** D. the oscillations of the electric field lie in a single plane
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-20 -->

Explanation: Polarisation restricts the transverse oscillations to one plane. It does not change wavelength or frequency.

**A230.** A. one half
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-21 -->

Explanation: Half of the randomly oriented oscillations (the components along the transmission axis) pass through. All of it would pass only if it was already polarised along the axis.

**A231.** B. Some light is transmitted
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-22 -->

Explanation: The middle filter rotates the plane of polarisation by 45°, so the light has a component along the axis of the last filter. Crossed filters alone block light, but the third filter changes this.

**A232.** C. partially polarised mostly in the horizontal plane, and the lenses have a vertical transmission axis
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-23 -->

Explanation: Reflection from a horizontal surface partially polarises light in the horizontal plane. Vertical-axis lenses block most of it, while light waves cannot be longitudinal.

**A233.** D. emit waves of the same frequency with a constant phase difference
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-24 -->

Explanation: Coherence requires the same frequency and a fixed phase relationship. Matching colour or brightness does not guarantee a constant phase difference.

**A234.** A. their light is not coherent, as the phase difference varies randomly
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-25 -->

Explanation: The atoms in each globe emit light with randomly changing phase, so there is no stable phase difference. Interference still occurs, but it averages out too rapidly to see.

**A235.** B. 400 nm
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-26 -->

Explanation: λ in water = λ in air / n = 532 / 1.33 = 400 nm. The frequency is unchanged, so the wavelength shrinks; 532 nm would imply no change.

**A236.** C. Red
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-27 -->

Explanation: Red has the longest wavelength of these, and diffraction is greatest when the wavelength is large compared with the slit width.

**A237.** Coherent sources emit waves of the same frequency with a constant phase difference [1]; both slits are illuminated by the same wavefront from one source, so they keep a fixed phase relationship and produce a stable interference pattern [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-28 -->

Explanation:

**A238.** Use Δx = λL/d [1]; Δx = (550 × 10⁻⁹ × 2.0) / (0.25 × 10⁻³) [1]; Δx = 4.4 × 10⁻³ m (4.4 mm) [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-29 -->

Explanation:

**A239.** Light diffracts at each slit so that waves spread out and overlap [1]; where the path difference is a whole number of wavelengths the waves arrive in phase and interfere constructively, giving a bright fringe [1]; where the path difference is an odd number of half wavelengths they arrive out of phase and interfere destructively, giving a dark fringe [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-30 -->

Explanation:

**A240.** Path difference in wavelengths = 2.4 × 10⁻⁶ / 6.00 × 10⁻⁷ = 4.0 [1]; a whole number of wavelengths means constructive interference, so P is a bright fringe [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-31 -->

Explanation:

**A241.** λ = c/f = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 1.00 × 10⁸ = 3.0 m [1]; diffraction is significant when the obstacle or opening is comparable in size to the wavelength [1]; radio wavelengths (metres) are comparable to buildings, whereas visible light (about 5 × 10⁻⁷ m) is far smaller, so it shows negligible diffraction [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-32 -->

Explanation:

**A242.** The fringe spacing decreases [1]; the frequency stays the same but the speed of light is lower in water, so the wavelength decreases [1]; since Δx = λL/d, a smaller λ gives a smaller fringe spacing [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-33 -->

Explanation:

**A243.** Rearrange Δx = λL/d to λ = Δx d / L [1]; λ = (3.6 × 10⁻³ × 3.0 × 10⁻⁴) / 1.8 [1]; λ = 6.0 × 10⁻⁷ m (600 nm) [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-wave-34 -->

Explanation:

**A244.** C. 3.0 m
<!-- id: vce-physics12-wm-c1 -->

Explanation: λ = c/f = 3.0 × 10⁸ ÷ 1.0 × 10⁸ = 3.0 m. Remember that mega means 10⁶.

#### The electromagnetic spectrum

**A245.** B. gamma rays
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-1 -->

Explanation: In order of increasing frequency: radio, microwave, infrared, visible, ultraviolet, X-ray, gamma.

**A246.** C. speed
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-2 -->

Explanation: All electromagnetic waves travel at c = 3.00 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹ in a vacuum. Frequency and wavelength differ across the spectrum.

**A247.** D. 3.00 m
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-3 -->

Explanation: λ = c/f = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 1.00 × 10⁸ = 3.00 m.

**A248.** B. X-rays: imaging bones
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-4 -->

Explanation: X-rays pass through soft tissue but are absorbed by bone, so they are used for bone imaging. Remote controls use infrared; fractures are imaged by X-rays rather than radio waves.

**A249.** C. visible
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-5 -->

Explanation: The visible region is roughly 400 nm to 700 nm; 450 nm is blue-violet visible light.

**A250.** (a) radio, microwave, infrared, visible, ultraviolet, X-ray, gamma (all in correct order for 2 marks); (b) c = fλ, so frequency is inversely proportional to wavelength (constant speed in a vacuum).
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-6 -->

Explanation:

**A251.** (a) f = c/λ = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 1.00 × 10⁻¹⁰ = 3.00 × 10¹⁸ Hz; (b) t = d/c = 1.50 × 10¹¹ / 3.00 × 10⁸ = 500 s (about 8.3 minutes).
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-7 -->

Explanation:

**A252.** Ultraviolet has a higher frequency (shorter wavelength) so each photon carries more energy; this energy can damage DNA and cells (e.g. sunburn, skin cancer) whereas visible light photons do not; protection: the ozone layer absorbs UV, or sunscreen, clothing and sunglasses absorb or block it.
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-8 -->

Explanation:

**A253.** B. radio waves
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-9 -->

Explanation: Wavelength increases as frequency decreases, so radio waves, at the low-frequency end of the spectrum, have the longest wavelengths. Microwaves are longer than infrared but shorter than radio.

**A254.** A. 5.66 × 10¹⁴ Hz
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-10 -->

Explanation: f = c/λ = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 5.30 × 10⁻⁷ = 5.66 × 10¹⁴ Hz. Multiplying c by λ instead of dividing gives a meaningless result.

**A255.** C. absorbed by water molecules in the food, increasing their thermal energy
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-11 -->

Explanation: Water molecules in the food absorb the microwave energy, which raises their thermal energy. Microwaves are non-ionising, so they do not ionise the food.

**A256.** D. infrared radiation emitted by the person
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-12 -->

Explanation: Objects at body temperature emit infrared radiation, which the camera detects. Reflected visible light is absent in the dark.

**A257.** B. 0.122 m
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-13 -->

Explanation: λ = c/f = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 2.45 × 10⁹ = 0.122 m. Dividing f by c gives 8.17, which is not a wavelength.

**A258.** A. halves
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-14 -->

Explanation: Since c = fλ and c is constant in a vacuum, wavelength is inversely proportional to frequency. Doubling f halves λ.

**A259.** C. ultrasound
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-15 -->

Explanation: Ultrasound is a mechanical sound wave that needs a medium, whereas electromagnetic waves can travel through a vacuum.

**A260.** D. transverse waves of oscillating electric and magnetic fields
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-16 -->

Explanation: Electromagnetic waves are transverse, with electric and magnetic fields oscillating perpendicular to each other and to the direction of travel. They are not particle streams and need no medium.

**A261.** A. 5.00 × 10⁻⁷ m
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-17 -->

Explanation: λ = c/f = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 6.00 × 10¹⁴ = 5.00 × 10⁻⁷ m (500 nm). Dividing f by c gives 2.00 × 10⁶, which is not a wavelength.

**A262.** C. 0.120 s
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-18 -->

Explanation: t = d/c = 3.60 × 10⁷ / 3.00 × 10⁸ = 0.120 s. Inverting the ratio gives 8.33, which is wrong.

**A263.** D. sterilising equipment and treating cancer
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-19 -->

Explanation: Gamma rays are highly energetic ionising radiation that can kill cells and microbes. Forged banknotes are checked with UV and satellite communication uses radio or microwaves.

**A264.** B. absorbs ultraviolet and re-emits it as visible light
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-20 -->

Explanation: Fluorescent inks absorb ultraviolet photons and re-emit lower-energy visible light. Re-emitting at a higher energy such as X-rays would be impossible.

**A265.** A. infrared
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-21 -->

Explanation: 10 μm = 1.0 × 10⁻⁵ m, which is longer than visible light (700 nm) but much shorter than microwaves, so it is infrared.

**A266.** C. microwaves
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-22 -->

Explanation: Mobile phones communicate using microwaves, which carry information over long distances through the atmosphere. Ultraviolet and gamma rays are ionising and unsuitable.

**A267.** A. gamma rays, ultraviolet, infrared, radio waves
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-23 -->

Explanation: Wavelength increases as frequency decreases, so the order is gamma, ultraviolet, infrared, radio. The second list is in order of decreasing wavelength.

**A268.** B. microwaves
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-24 -->

Explanation: λ = c/f = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 1.0 × 10¹⁰ = 0.030 m (3 cm), which is the microwave region. Infrared and visible light have wavelengths of micrometres or less.

**A269.** They all travel at the same speed, c = 3.00 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹ [1]; they are all transverse waves of oscillating electric and magnetic fields (and can travel through a vacuum) [1]; frequency (or wavelength, or photon energy) differs between regions [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-25 -->

Explanation:

**A270.** All objects above absolute zero emit infrared radiation, with warmer objects emitting more, due to the thermal motion of their particles [1]; application, e.g. thermal imaging cameras, remote controls or infrared heaters [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-26 -->

Explanation:

**A271.** Ionising radiation has photons with enough energy to remove electrons from atoms or molecules [1]; non-ionising radiation does not have enough energy to do so [1]; examples: ionising: ultraviolet (high end), X-rays or gamma rays; non-ionising: radio, microwave, infrared or visible [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-27 -->

Explanation:

**A272.** X-rays are directed through the arm onto a detector or film [1]; bone absorbs more X-rays than soft tissue, so fewer X-rays reach the detector behind the bone [1]; this produces contrast, with the bone appearing as a different brightness from the surrounding tissue, so the fracture is visible [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-28 -->

Explanation:

**A273.** Total distance travelled = ct = 3.00 × 10⁸ × 4.0 × 10⁻⁴ = 1.2 × 10⁵ m [1]; this is a round trip, so divide by 2 [1]; distance = 6.0 × 10⁴ m (60 km) [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-29 -->

Explanation:

**A274.** Gamma rays have a much higher frequency than radio waves [1]; gamma rays have a much shorter wavelength than radio waves [1]; gamma-ray photons carry much more energy than radio-wave photons [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-30 -->

Explanation:

**A275.** Sterilising water or equipment, because ultraviolet photons have enough energy to damage the DNA of microbes [1]; detecting forged banknotes (or fluorescence), because certain materials absorb ultraviolet and re-emit visible light [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-31 -->

Explanation:

**A276.** f = c/λ [1]; violet: 3.00 × 10⁸ / 4.00 × 10⁻⁷ = 7.50 × 10¹⁴ Hz [1]; red: 3.00 × 10⁸ / 7.00 × 10⁻⁷ = 4.29 × 10¹⁴ Hz [1]; violet has the higher frequency (shorter wavelength) [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-32 -->

Explanation:

**A277.** The claim is incorrect: all electromagnetic waves, including radio and visible light, travel at the same speed c = 3.00 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹ in a vacuum [1]; radio waves are used for communication because of their long wavelengths and how they interact with the atmosphere, not because they are faster [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-33 -->

Explanation:

**A278.** λ = c/f [1]; 5G: 3.00 × 10⁸ / 2.80 × 10¹⁰ = 1.07 × 10⁻² m [1]; older: 3.00 × 10⁸ / 7.00 × 10⁸ = 0.429 m [1].
<!-- id: physics12a1-em-34 -->

Explanation:

**A279.** C. Infrared
<!-- id: vce-physics12-em-c1 -->

Explanation: In order of increasing wavelength: gamma, X-rays, UV, visible, infrared, microwaves, radio.

#### Refraction and Snell's law

**A280.** D. 25.4°
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-1 -->

Explanation: sin θ₂ = (1.00 × sin 40°)/1.50 = 0.6428/1.50 = 0.4285, so θ₂ = 25.4°. 74.6° results from multiplying instead of dividing by n.

**A281.** A. 1.33
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-2 -->

Explanation: n = c/v = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 2.25 × 10⁸ = 1.33.

**A282.** C. bends away from the normal
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-3 -->

Explanation: Light speeds up as it enters the lower-index medium (air), so it bends away from the normal.

**A283.** B. travels from a medium of higher refractive index to one of lower refractive index at an angle of incidence greater than the critical angle
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-4 -->

Explanation: TIR requires travel from higher n to lower n with an angle of incidence exceeding the critical angle.

**A284.** D. 41.8°
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-5 -->

Explanation: sin θc = n₂/n₁ = 1.00/1.50 = 0.667, so θc = 41.8°. 48.2° is 90° − 41.8°.

**A285.** (a) sin θ₂ = sin 30.0° / 1.33 = 0.3759, θ₂ = 22.1°; (b) v = c/n = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 1.33 = 2.26 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹; (c) towards the normal, as light slows in the higher-index medium.
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-6 -->

Explanation:

**A286.** (a) sin θc = 1/2.42 = 0.413, so θc = 24.4°; (b) the critical angle is small so light entering the diamond strikes internal surfaces at angles larger than θc and is totally internally reflected many times before exiting through certain faces, producing intense sparkle.
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-7 -->

Explanation:

**A287.** Rays from a point on the bottom bend away from the normal as they pass from water into air (lower n); the eye traces the rays back in straight lines to an apparent source position; this apparent position is higher than the real position, so the pool looks shallower.
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-8 -->

Explanation:

**A288.** B. 32.1°
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-9 -->

Explanation: sin θ₂ = sin 45° / 1.33 = 0.7071/1.33 = 0.5316, so θ₂ = 32.1°. 70.1° results from multiplying by 1.33 instead of dividing.

**A289.** C. Frequency
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-10 -->

Explanation: Frequency is set by the source and stays constant across a boundary. Speed and wavelength both decrease in glass, which is what causes the ray to change direction.

**A290.** D. 400 nm
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-11 -->

Explanation: Frequency is unchanged and v = c/n, so λ = λ₀/n = 600/1.50 = 400 nm. 900 nm results from multiplying by n instead of dividing.

**A291.** A. n₁ sin θ₁ = n₂ sin θ₂
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-12 -->

Explanation: Snell's law relates the sines of the angles to the refractive indices as n₁ sin θ₁ = n₂ sin θ₂. The angles themselves are not in simple proportion, so the last option is wrong.

**A292.** B. 26.3°
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-13 -->

Explanation: sin θ₂ = 1.33 × sin 30.0° / 1.50 = 0.4433, so θ₂ = 26.3°. 34.3° comes from inverting the ratio of refractive indices.

**A293.** C. 48.8°
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-14 -->

Explanation: sin θc = 1.00/1.33 = 0.752, so θc = 48.8°. 41.2° is 90° − 48.8°, the complement.

**A294.** D. the core has a higher refractive index than the cladding, so light is totally internally reflected
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-15 -->

Explanation: Light in the higher-index core meets the core–cladding boundary above the critical angle and is totally internally reflected. A lower-index core could never produce total internal reflection.

**A295.** A. along the boundary, at 90° to the normal
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-16 -->

Explanation: The critical angle is the angle of incidence for which the angle of refraction is 90°, so the ray skims along the boundary. Beyond it there is no refracted ray.

**A296.** B. 1.51
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-17 -->

Explanation: n = sin 60° / sin 35° = 0.8660/0.5736 = 1.51. Dividing the angles directly (60/35 = 1.71) is not valid because Snell's law uses sines.

**A297.** C. Speed and wavelength both decrease
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-18 -->

Explanation: Light slows in glass and, because frequency is fixed, v = fλ means the wavelength shortens too. Frequency does not decrease.

**A298.** D. Light travels slower in Y than in X
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-19 -->

Explanation: Bending towards the normal means Y has the higher refractive index, so light is slower in Y. Frequency stays the same across the boundary.

**A299.** A. passes into the glass without changing direction but slows down
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-20 -->

Explanation: At 0° incidence sin θ₂ = 0, so the direction is unchanged, but the speed still falls to c/n. Total internal reflection only occurs from higher to lower refractive index.

**A300.** B. 62.5°
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-21 -->

Explanation: sin θc = 1.33/1.50 = 0.887, so θc = 62.5°. 41.8° is the glass–air value, which does not apply to a glass–water boundary.

**A301.** D. 48.6°
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-22 -->

Explanation: sin θ₂ = 1.50 × sin 30.0° / 1.00 = 0.750, so θ₂ = 48.6°. 19.5° comes from dividing by 1.50 rather than multiplying.

**A302.** A. parallel to the incident ray but displaced sideways
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-23 -->

Explanation: The two refractions are equal and opposite, so the final direction matches the original, but the ray is shifted sideways. It is only in line with the original ray when incident along the normal.

**A303.** B. It is totally internally reflected, leaving at 45° to the normal
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-24 -->

Explanation: 45° exceeds the critical angle of 41.8°, so no light is refracted. The ray is totally internally reflected and the angle of reflection equals the angle of incidence.

**A304.** C. light travels fastest in a vacuum, so v is never greater than c
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-25 -->

Explanation: Since n = c/v and v cannot exceed c in a material, n is at least 1. Frequency does not change at a boundary, so the second option is wrong.

**A305.** sin θ₂ = (1.00 × sin 35.0°)/1.52 = 0.5736/1.52 = 0.3774 [1]; θ₂ = 22.2° [1]
