<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-26 -->

Explanation:

**A306.** (a) v = c/n = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 1.52 = 1.97 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹ [1]; (b) frequency is unchanged, so λ = λ₀/n = 589/1.52 [1] = 388 nm [1]
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-27 -->

Explanation:

**A307.** Light travels more slowly in glass than in air (higher refractive index) [1]; the part of the wavefront that enters the glass first slows down first, so the wavefront swings and the ray bends towards the normal [1]
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-28 -->

Explanation:

**A308.** (a) sin θc = 1.00/1.60 = 0.625 [1], θc = 38.7° [1]; (b) light must travel from the higher-index glass towards air, and the angle of incidence must exceed the critical angle [1]
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-29 -->

Explanation:

**A309.** sin θ₂ = 1.33 × sin 40.0° / 1.50 = 1.33 × 0.6428/1.50 = 0.5700 [1]; θ₂ = 34.7° [1]
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-30 -->

Explanation:

**A310.** Shine a narrow ray from the ray box into the block at several different angles of incidence, tracing the incident and emergent rays and the block outline on paper [1]; draw the normal and join the entry and exit points to find the refracted ray inside the block [1]; measure the angles of incidence and refraction with a protractor for each trial [1]; plot sin θ₁ against sin θ₂ (or calculate sin θ₁/sin θ₂ for each trial) and the gradient (average) gives the refractive index of the glass [1]
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-31 -->

Explanation:

**A311.** The fibre has a core of higher refractive index surrounded by cladding of lower refractive index [1]; light enters the core and strikes the core–cladding boundary at an angle of incidence greater than the critical angle [1]; it is totally internally reflected repeatedly, so none escapes and it travels along the fibre [1]
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-32 -->

Explanation:

**A312.** Light from the submerged part of the straw bends away from the normal as it passes from water into air [1]; the eye traces these rays back in straight lines, so the submerged part appears at a different (shallower) position from the part in air, making the straw look bent [1]
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-33 -->

Explanation:

**A313.** (a) sin θ₂ = 1.50 × sin 55.0° / 1.33 = 1.50 × 0.8192/1.33 = 0.9238 [1], θ₂ = 67.5° [1]; (b) no, because the angle of incidence (55.0°) is less than the critical angle sin⁻¹(1.33/1.50) = 62.5° [1]
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-34 -->

Explanation:

**A314.** Trial 1: n = sin 20.0°/sin 13.0° = 0.3420/0.2250 = 1.52 [1]; Trial 2: n = sin 40.0°/sin 25.0° = 0.6428/0.4226 = 1.52 [1]; average refractive index = 1.52 [1]
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-35 -->

Explanation:

**A315.** (a) λ = c/f = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 5.00 × 10¹⁴ = 6.00 × 10⁻⁷ m [1]; (b) v = c/n = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 1.33 = 2.26 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹ [1]; (c) λ = v/f = 2.26 × 10⁸ / 5.00 × 10¹⁴ = 4.51 × 10⁻⁷ m [1]; (d) frequency is unchanged, 5.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz [1]
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-36 -->

Explanation:

**A316.** The refractive index of glass is slightly different for different wavelengths, being larger for violet/blue light than for red light [1]; so on entering the prism each colour is refracted through a different angle (violet bends most, red least) [1]; the separation increases at the second face, producing a spectrum [1]
<!-- id: physics12a1-ref-37 -->

Explanation:

**A317.** About 25°
<!-- id: vce-physics12-rs-c1 -->

Explanation: n₁ sin θ₁ = n₂ sin θ₂: 1.00 × sin 40° = 1.50 × sin θ₂, so sin θ₂ = 0.429 and θ₂ ≈ 25.4°. Light bends towards the normal entering a denser medium.

#### Total internal reflection and dispersion

**A318.** D. 41.8°
<!-- id: physics12a2-tir-1 -->

Explanation: sin θc = n₂/n₁ = 1.00/1.50 = 0.667, so θc = 41.8°. 48.2° is the complement (90° − 41.8°), a common slip.

**A319.** B. Light moves from a more dense to a less dense medium, and the angle of incidence is greater than the critical angle
<!-- id: physics12a2-tir-2 -->

Explanation: TIR needs the light to be in the higher-refractive-index medium heading towards the lower one, with the angle of incidence exceeding θc. Going into a denser medium the ray always bends towards the normal, so no critical angle exists.

**A320.** A. glass has a higher refractive index for violet light than for red light
<!-- id: physics12a2-tir-3 -->

Explanation: Refractive index depends on wavelength: n is larger for violet (shorter wavelength), so violet travels slower in glass and bends more on refraction. Violet has a higher, not lower, frequency and a shorter wavelength.

**A321.** C. 28.9°
<!-- id: physics12a2-tir-4 -->

Explanation: sin θ₂ = sin 40° / 1.33 = 0.6428 / 1.33 = 0.483, so θ₂ = 28.9°. 30.1° comes from dividing the angle (40°) by 1.33 rather than the sine; 58.8° comes from multiplying by 1.33.

**A322.** B. light in the core undergoes total internal reflection at the core-cladding boundary
<!-- id: physics12a2-tir-5 -->

Explanation: TIR requires the light to be in the higher-index medium meeting a lower-index one at an angle beyond θc. The lower-index cladding provides this at the boundary, so the signal stays in the core.

**A323.** (a) sin θc = 1/2.42 = 0.413, so θc ≈ 24.4°; (b) the critical angle is small, so most rays inside the diamond strike internal faces at angles greater than θc; they undergo total internal reflection several times before leaving through the top, giving bright sparkle (and dispersion adds colour).
<!-- id: physics12a2-tir-6 -->

Explanation:

**A324.** (a) sin θc = 1.33/1.52 = 0.875, so θc ≈ 61°; (b) at 55° (< θc) the ray is partly refracted into the water, bending away from the normal, with a weak partial reflection; at 65° (> θc) there is total internal reflection, so no light enters the water.
<!-- id: physics12a2-tir-7 -->

Explanation:

**A325.** White light is a mixture of wavelengths; the refractive index of the glass or water depends on wavelength, being greater for shorter wavelengths; so each colour refracts by a different amount and separates (violet bends most, red least); in a raindrop the red light is deviated least, so it reaches the observer from the higher, outer part of the bow.
<!-- id: physics12a2-tir-8 -->

Explanation:

**A326.** C. 42°
<!-- id: vce-physics12-ti-c1 -->

Explanation: sin c = n₂/n₁ = 1 ÷ 1.50, so c ≈ 41.8°. Diamond (n = 2.42) has c ≈ 24°, which is why it sparkles.

### Unit 1: Nuclear energy

#### Nuclear stability and isotopes

**A327.** C. They have the same number of protons but different numbers of neutrons
<!-- id: physics12a2-nsi-1 -->

Explanation: An element is defined by its proton number Z. Isotopes share Z but differ in neutron number N, and therefore in mass number A.

**A328.** C. 143
<!-- id: physics12a2-nsi-2 -->

Explanation: N = A − Z = 235 − 92 = 143. 92 is the proton number and 235 is the total number of nucleons.

**A329.** B. the strong nuclear force, which acts attractively between nucleons over very short distances
<!-- id: physics12a2-nsi-3 -->

Explanation: The strong nuclear force is attractive between nucleons, much stronger than electrostatic repulsion at about 1 fm separation, but has a very short range. Gravity is far too weak at this scale.

**A330.** C. greater than one
<!-- id: physics12a2-nsi-4 -->

Explanation: Heavy stable nuclei have more neutrons than protons (N/Z up to about 1.5). Extra neutrons add strong-force attraction without adding electrostatic repulsion, which grows with the number of protons.

**A331.** A. Iron-56
<!-- id: physics12a2-nsi-5 -->

Explanation: The curve peaks near iron-56 (about 8.8 MeV per nucleon), so it is among the most tightly bound nuclei. Uranium-235 sits lower (about 7.6 MeV) and hydrogen-2 much lower (about 1.1 MeV).

**A332.** (a) E = 0.0304 × 931.5 ≈ 28.3 MeV; (b) 4 nucleons, so 28.3 / 4 ≈ 7.08 MeV per nucleon (≈ 7.1 MeV).
<!-- id: physics12a2-nsi-6 -->

Explanation:

**A333.** (a) each has 6 protons; neutrons 6, 7 and 8 respectively; (b) ¹⁴C has too many neutrons for its number of protons (N/Z above the stable band), so the nucleus is unstable; it changes by decay (beta-minus) to reach a more stable ratio.
<!-- id: physics12a2-nsi-7 -->

Explanation:

**A334.** The strong force is attractive but very short range, so each nucleon only attracts its near neighbours; the electrostatic repulsion between protons has a long range and acts between all proton pairs, so it grows faster with size; extra neutrons add strong-force attraction and spread the protons apart without adding repulsion; so heavy nuclei need N > Z to be stable.
<!-- id: physics12a2-nsi-8 -->

Explanation:

**A335.** C. Extra neutrons add strong-force attraction without adding electrostatic repulsion
<!-- id: vce-physics12-ns-c1 -->

Explanation: The strong force acts only over very short distances, while proton–proton repulsion acts across the whole nucleus.

#### Alpha, beta and gamma radiation

**A336.** C. Alpha radiation
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-1 -->

Explanation: Alpha particles (helium nuclei) are heavy and highly ionising, so they lose their energy within a few centimetres of air or in paper. Beta needs millimetres of aluminium and gamma needs thick lead or concrete.

**A337.** A. radon-222 (Z = 86)
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-2 -->

Explanation: An alpha particle is ⁴₂He, so A falls by 4 (226 → 222) and Z falls by 2 (88 → 86). Element 86 is radon.

**A338.** B. A = 60 and Z = 28
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-3 -->

Explanation: In β⁻ decay a neutron becomes a proton, an electron and an antineutrino. A stays at 60 and Z rises by 1 to 28 (nickel-60).

**A339.** B. changes neither its mass number nor its atomic number
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-4 -->

Explanation: Gamma emission releases excess energy from an excited nucleus, usually after alpha or beta decay. The number and type of nucleons stay the same, so A and Z are unchanged.

**A340.** D. beta radiation only
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-5 -->

Explanation: Paper not reducing the count rules out alpha. Aluminium removing nearly everything rules out significant gamma, which would still penetrate it. This matches beta radiation alone.

**A341.** (a) ²⁴¹₉₅Am → ²³⁷₉₃Np + ⁴₂He; (b) ¹⁴₆C → ¹⁴₇N + ⁰₋₁e + antineutrino (ν̄); mass numbers and atomic numbers balance on both sides in each equation.
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-6 -->

Explanation:

**A342.** Alpha: greatest ionising ability (large mass and +2 charge), least penetrating (stopped by paper or a few cm of air); beta: moderate ionising ability, moderate penetration (stopped by a few mm of aluminium); gamma: weakest ionising ability (no charge), most penetrating (needs thick lead or concrete); ionising ability and penetrating power are inversely related.
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-7 -->

Explanation:

**A343.** Alpha particles strongly ionise the air in the chamber, producing a steady current between the plates; smoke particles absorb or attach to the ions and reduce the current, triggering the alarm; alpha radiation is stopped by the detector casing and a few cm of air, so little escapes; the source is only dangerous if ingested or inhaled.
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-8 -->

Explanation:

**A344.** A. a helium-4 nucleus
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-9 -->

Explanation: An alpha particle consists of two protons and two neutrons, which is a helium-4 nucleus. A fast-moving electron is a beta-minus particle, not an alpha particle.

**A345.** B. a high-speed electron emitted from the nucleus
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-10 -->

Explanation: A β⁻ particle is an electron created when a neutron changes into a proton. It is not an electromagnetic wave, which describes gamma radiation.

**A346.** C. high-energy electromagnetic radiation
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-11 -->

Explanation: Gamma rays are very high-frequency photons with no mass and no charge. They are not particles like alpha or beta, which is why they are the most penetrating.

**A347.** D. thorium-234 (Z = 90)
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-12 -->

Explanation: Emitting ⁴₂He reduces A from 238 to 234 and Z from 92 to 90, giving thorium-234. Keeping A at 238 would ignore the mass carried away by the alpha particle.

**A348.** A. yttrium-90 (Z = 39)
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-13 -->

Explanation: In β⁻ decay A is unchanged and Z increases by 1, so the daughter has A = 90 and Z = 39 (yttrium). Rubidium would result from a decrease in Z, which does not happen in β⁻ decay.

**A349.** B. the opposite sign of charge and a much smaller mass
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-14 -->

Explanation: A beta-minus particle is a negative electron with charge −e, whereas an alpha particle has charge +2e. The electron's mass is thousands of times smaller than that of an alpha particle.

**A350.** C. Gamma radiation
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-15 -->

Explanation: Gamma rays carry no charge, so a magnetic field exerts no force on them. Alpha and beta are charged and are deflected in opposite directions.

**A351.** D. oxygen-18 (Z = 8)
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-16 -->

Explanation: In β⁺ decay a proton becomes a neutron, so A stays at 18 and Z falls by 1 to 8 (oxygen-18). Neon-18 would require Z to increase, as in β⁻ decay.

**A352.** A. inhaled or swallowed
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-17 -->

Explanation: Inside the body alpha particles deposit all their energy in a tiny volume of living tissue, causing intense ionisation. Outside the body they are stopped by clothing, paper or the outer layer of skin.

**A353.** B. A thick block of lead
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-18 -->

Explanation: Gamma rays are highly penetrating and need dense material such as thick lead or concrete to attenuate them. Paper and thin aluminium would block alpha and some beta, but barely affect gamma.

**A354.** D. decreases by 2
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-19 -->

Explanation: An alpha particle carries away 2 protons and 2 neutrons, so N falls by 2 as well as Z. The mass number falls by 4, which is not the same as the neutron number.

**A355.** A. a neutron changes into a proton
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-20 -->

Explanation: A neutron converts into a proton, an electron (the beta particle) and an antineutrino. The reverse change, proton to neutron, occurs in beta-plus decay.

**A356.** B. the daughter nucleus is left in an excited state
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-21 -->

Explanation: The daughter nucleus is often formed in an excited energy state and releases the excess energy as a gamma photon. This changes the energy of the nucleus but not its number of protons or neutrons.

**A357.** C. have a larger charge and move more slowly
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-22 -->

Explanation: Their +2e charge and much greater mass mean they interact strongly with atoms and give up energy over a very short path. A beta particle is lighter and faster, so it is less ionising.

**A358.** D. a beta emitter, because the count rate changes with small changes in thickness
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-23 -->

Explanation: Beta particles are partly absorbed by paper, so the detected count varies as the thickness varies. Alpha would be fully stopped and gamma would pass almost unaffected, giving no useful signal.

**A359.** A. A = 228 and Z = 90
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-24 -->

Explanation: The alpha decay gives A = 228, Z = 88, and each β⁻ decay then raises Z by 1 without changing A, ending at Z = 90. The beta decays do not alter the mass number.

**A360.** B. it allows energy and momentum to be conserved
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-25 -->

Explanation: Beta particles are emitted with a range of energies, so a third particle must share the available energy and momentum. The antineutrino has no charge and almost no mass, so it does not affect A or Z balance.

**A361.** C. toward the negative plate
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-26 -->

Explanation: Alpha particles carry positive charge, so they are attracted to the negative plate. Beta-minus particles would be deflected the other way, toward the positive plate.

**A362.** Measure the count rate with no absorber and subtract background radiation [1] place each absorber in turn between source and detector; a drop with paper indicates alpha, a drop with aluminium indicates beta [1] a count that remains until thick lead is added indicates gamma [1]
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-27 -->

Explanation:

**A363.** Alpha particles are massive, slow and carry a +2 charge, so they interact strongly with atoms and strip electrons from them [1] this means they lose their energy in a very short distance and are stopped by paper or a few cm of air [1] gamma photons are uncharged and interact rarely with matter, so they ionise little per unit distance but travel far through dense materials [1]
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-28 -->

Explanation:

**A364.** ²²²₈₆Rn → ²¹⁸₈₄Po + ⁴₂He with mass numbers and atomic numbers balanced [1]
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-29 -->

Explanation:

**A365.** Alpha particles are positively charged and are deflected in one direction along a curved path [1] beta-minus particles are negatively charged and are deflected in the opposite direction, with a tighter curve because of their much smaller mass [1] gamma rays have no charge so they are not deflected [1]
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-30 -->

Explanation:

**A366.** ²¹⁴₈₃Bi → ²¹⁴₈₄Po + ⁰₋₁e + antineutrino (ν̄) [1] polonium-214 with Z = 84 identified as the daughter [1] ²¹⁴₈₄Po → ²¹⁰₈₂Pb + ⁴₂He [1] lead-210 with Z = 82 identified, with A and Z balanced in both equations [1]
<!-- id: physics12a2-abg-31 -->

Explanation:

**A367.** A. Protactinium-234 (Z = 91)
<!-- id: vce-physics12-ab-c1 -->

Explanation: In β⁻ decay a neutron becomes a proton and an electron is emitted: A stays 234, Z rises by 1.

#### Half-life and activity

**A368.** A. 5.0 g
<!-- id: physics12a2-hla-1 -->

Explanation: 24 h is 4 half-lives: 80 → 40 → 20 → 10 → 5.0 g. Using 2 half-lives (20 g) or 3 (10 g) means mis-counting the number of halvings.

**A369.** B. half of the nuclei in a sample to decay
<!-- id: physics12a2-hla-2 -->

Explanation: Half-life is the time for half of the radioactive nuclei present to decay, or equivalently for the activity to halve. It is not the time for everything to decay.

**A370.** D. 5.0 days
<!-- id: physics12a2-hla-3 -->

Explanation: 1200 → 600 → 300 → 150 is 3 half-lives, so t½ = 15 / 3 = 5.0 days.

**A371.** A. 1 nucleus decays per second
<!-- id: physics12a2-hla-4 -->

Explanation: The becquerel is one decay per second. Joules per kilogram describes the gray, which measures absorbed dose, not activity.

**A372.** C. It is impossible to predict when any one nucleus will decay, but the fraction decaying in a given time is predictable
<!-- id: physics12a2-hla-5 -->

Explanation: Decay is random for each nucleus, but with many nuclei the decay rate follows a predictable exponential pattern with a fixed half-life. Half-life is not changed by temperature or by how long a nucleus has already survived.

**A373.** (a) 24 days is 3 half-lives: 64 → 32 → 16 → 8.0 MBq; (b) 64 to 2.0 MBq is a factor of 32 = 2⁵, so 5 half-lives; 5 × 8.0 = 40 days.
<!-- id: physics12a2-hla-6 -->

Explanation:

**A374.** 25% is two halvings (100 → 50 → 25), so 2 half-lives; age ≈ 2 × 5730 = 11 460 years (about 1.1 × 10⁴ years); assumption: the ratio of carbon-14 in living organisms (atmosphere) was constant over time, so the original activity equals that of living wood.
<!-- id: physics12a2-hla-7 -->

Explanation:

**A375.** (a) 24 h is 4 half-lives, (1/2)⁴ = 1/16 ≈ 6.25%; (b) long enough for the imaging to be carried out after preparation and delivery; short enough that the activity (and the patient's dose) falls quickly so it does not stay in the body for long.
<!-- id: physics12a2-hla-8 -->

Explanation:

**A376.** B. 6 hours
<!-- id: vce-physics12-hl-c1 -->

Explanation: 800 → 400 → 200 → 100 → 50 is 4 half-lives in 24 hours, so the half-life is 6 hours.

#### Nuclear fission and fusion

**A377.** C. A heavy nucleus splits into two smaller nuclei, usually after absorbing a neutron
<!-- id: physics12a2-nff-1 -->

Explanation: Fission is the splitting of a heavy nucleus such as uranium-235 into two medium-sized fragments plus neutrons, releasing energy. Joining light nuclei is fusion.

**A378.** D. the nuclei must have enough kinetic energy to overcome their electrostatic repulsion and get close enough for the strong force to act
<!-- id: physics12a2-nff-2 -->

Explanation: Positively charged nuclei repel each other, so they must collide at very high speeds (temperatures of millions of kelvin) to approach within about 1 fm where the strong nuclear force takes over.

**A379.** C. the products have a greater binding energy per nucleon than the reactants
<!-- id: physics12a2-nff-3 -->

Explanation: In each case the nuclei move towards the peak of the binding energy curve. The products are more tightly bound, so total mass falls (a mass defect) and the difference appears as energy. The number of nucleons is conserved.

**A380.** B. absorb neutrons to regulate the rate of the chain reaction
<!-- id: physics12a2-nff-4 -->

Explanation: Control rods (for example boron or cadmium) absorb neutrons. Moving them in or out changes how many neutrons go on to cause fission. Slowing neutrons is the role of the moderator.

**A381.** C. Exactly one
<!-- id: physics12a2-nff-5 -->

Explanation: Each fission releases two or three neutrons, but in steady operation the average number that go on to cause another fission is exactly one. More than one gives an accelerating chain reaction; less than one lets it die away.

**A382.** (a) E = 0.20 × 931.5 = 186 MeV (186.3); (b) 186.3 × 10⁶ eV × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ J eV⁻¹ ≈ 2.98 × 10⁻¹¹ J (about 3.0 × 10⁻¹¹ J).
<!-- id: physics12a2-nff-6 -->

Explanation:

**A383.** Fission: heavy nuclei (e.g. U-235) split, started by neutron absorption, at moderate temperatures, producing radioactive fission products that need long-term storage; fusion: light nuclei (e.g. H-2 and H-3) join, requiring extremely high temperature and pressure, producing helium and neutrons with much less long-lived waste; both release energy because binding energy per nucleon increases.
<!-- id: physics12a2-nff-7 -->

Explanation:

**A384.** A neutron is absorbed by a U-235 nucleus, which splits and releases two or three more neutrons; these can cause further fissions (a chain reaction); a moderator slows the neutrons so they are more likely to be absorbed; control rods absorb excess neutrons so that, on average, one neutron per fission causes another fission and the power stays steady.
<!-- id: physics12a2-nff-8 -->

Explanation:

**A385.** About 2.8 × 10⁻¹¹ J
<!-- id: vce-physics12-nf-c1 -->

Explanation: E = mc² = 3.1 × 10⁻²⁸ × (3.0 × 10⁸)² ≈ 2.8 × 10⁻¹¹ J. The missing mass (mass defect) is converted to energy.

#### Radiation dose and biological effects

**A386.** C. one joule per kilogram
<!-- id: physics12a2-rdb-1 -->

Explanation: Absorbed dose is energy absorbed per unit mass, so 1 Gy = 1 J kg⁻¹. One decay per second is the becquerel; one joule per coulomb is the volt.

**A387.** D. 0.080 Gy
<!-- id: physics12a2-rdb-2 -->

Explanation: D = E/m = 0.40 / 5.0 = 0.080 Gy. 12.5 Gy is the result of inverting the ratio (5.0 / 0.40).

**A388.** D. deposit their energy over a very short path, producing dense ionisation
<!-- id: physics12a2-rdb-3 -->

Explanation: Alpha particles are heavily ionising, depositing all their energy in a tiny volume of tissue, so they cause concentrated damage. This is reflected in their larger radiation weighting factor when equivalent dose is calculated.

**A389.** B. 10 µSv h⁻¹
<!-- id: physics12a2-rdb-4 -->

Explanation: Intensity from a point source follows an inverse-square law. Doubling the distance reduces the dose rate to one quarter: 40 / 4 = 10 µSv h⁻¹. 20 µSv h⁻¹ would result from an inverse (not inverse-square) relationship.

**A390.** A. An increased risk of cancer years later
<!-- id: physics12a2-rdb-5 -->

Explanation: Damage to DNA that is not repaired can lead to cancer many years later, with the probability rising with the total dose received. Burns, nausea and sickness are acute effects of high doses.

**A391.** (a) D = E/m = 2.5 × 10⁻³ / 50 = 5.0 × 10⁻⁵ Gy; (b) H = wR × D = 20 × 5.0 × 10⁻⁵ = 1.0 × 10⁻³ Sv (1.0 mSv).
<!-- id: physics12a2-rdb-6 -->

Explanation:

**A392.** (a) reduce the time of exposure, increase the distance, use shielding (e.g. lead); (b) at 2.0 m the dose rate is 80 / 2² = 20 µSv h⁻¹; dose = 20 × 3.0 = 60 µSv.
<!-- id: physics12a2-rdb-7 -->

Explanation:

**A393.** Ionising radiation removes electrons from atoms and molecules in cells, producing ions and free radicals that can break DNA strands or alter bases; damaged cells may die, mutate or become cancerous if the damage is not repaired; rapidly dividing cells (such as tumour cells) are more sensitive; radiotherapy aims a high dose at the tumour from several directions to kill cancer cells while limiting the dose to surrounding healthy tissue.
<!-- id: physics12a2-rdb-8 -->

Explanation:

**A394.** D. 40 mSv
<!-- id: vce-physics12-rd-c1 -->

Explanation: Equivalent dose = absorbed dose × weighting factor = 2.0 × 20 = 40 mSv. Alpha particles are highly ionising inside the body.

### Unit 1: Electricity

#### Charge, current and potential difference

**A395.** A. 60 C
<!-- id: physics12a2-cpd-1 -->

Explanation: Q = I t = 3.0 × 20 = 60 C. Dividing (20 / 3.0 = 6.7) is the wrong operation.

**A396.** B. 60 J
<!-- id: physics12a2-cpd-2 -->

Explanation: The potential difference is energy per unit charge, so W = qV = 5.0 × 12 = 60 J.

**A397.** C. from the positive terminal to the negative terminal outside the cell
<!-- id: physics12a2-cpd-3 -->

Explanation: By convention, current is defined as the flow of positive charge, from the positive terminal through the external circuit to the negative terminal. The electrons move in the opposite direction.

**A398.** C. 3.0 × 10¹⁹
<!-- id: physics12a2-cpd-4 -->

Explanation: n = Q / e = 4.8 / (1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹) = 3.0 × 10¹⁹ electrons.

**A399.** C. the ammeter is in series with the lamp and the voltmeter is in parallel with it
<!-- id: physics12a2-cpd-5 -->

Explanation: The ammeter has very low resistance and must carry the lamp's current, so it goes in series. The voltmeter has very high resistance and measures the difference between two points, so it goes in parallel.

**A400.** (a) Q = I t = 0.25 × 120 = 30 C; (b) n = 30 / 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ ≈ 1.9 × 10²⁰ electrons; (c) W = qV = 30 × 6.0 = 180 J.
<!-- id: physics12a2-cpd-6 -->

Explanation:

**A401.** Current is the rate of flow of charge past a point (I = Q/t), measured in amperes and measured in series with a component; potential difference is the energy transferred per unit charge between two points (V = W/q), measured in volts and measured in parallel across a component; current is a flow through the component while potential difference is across it (the supply provides the energy, charge carriers do not get used up).
<!-- id: physics12a2-cpd-7 -->

Explanation:

**A402.** (a) I = Q/t = 90 / 300 = 0.30 A; (b) V = W/q = 540 / 90 = 6.0 V; (c) P = VI = 6.0 × 0.30 = 1.8 W (also 540 / 300 = 1.8 W).
<!-- id: physics12a2-cpd-8 -->

Explanation:

**A403.** 360 C; about 2.3 × 10²¹ electrons
<!-- id: vce-physics12-cp-c1 -->

Explanation: Q = It = 2.0 × 180 = 360 C. Number of electrons = 360 ÷ 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ ≈ 2.25 × 10²¹. Convert minutes to seconds.

#### Resistance and Ohm's law

**A404.** A. 30 Ω
<!-- id: physics12a2-rol-1 -->

Explanation: R = V / I = 9.0 / 0.30 = 30 Ω. 2.7 comes from multiplying V by I, which gives power.

**A405.** C. R/2
<!-- id: physics12a2-rol-2 -->

Explanation: R is proportional to length and inversely proportional to cross-sectional area. Twice the length gives ×2; twice the diameter gives four times the area, so ×1/4. Net factor is 2/4 = 1/2, giving R/2.

**A406.** C. a straight line through the origin
<!-- id: physics12a2-rol-3 -->

Explanation: For an ohmic conductor I is proportional to V, so the I–V graph is a straight line through the origin; the gradient is 1/R. Curves are typical of non-ohmic devices.

**A407.** D. the higher temperature causes more frequent collisions between charge carriers and the vibrating lattice
<!-- id: physics12a2-rol-4 -->

Explanation: A larger current heats the filament, increasing lattice vibrations, so conduction electrons collide more often and the resistance rises. The filament is therefore non-ohmic.

**A408.** C. 36 W
<!-- id: physics12a2-rol-5 -->

Explanation: P = V²/R = 12² / 4.0 = 36 W (check: I = 3.0 A, P = VI = 36 W). 48 W comes from V × R.

**A409.** (a) length ×3 gives ×3; half the diameter gives one quarter the area, so resistance ×4; R = 6.0 × 3 × 4 = 72 Ω; (b) R is directly proportional to length and inversely proportional to cross-sectional area (area proportional to diameter squared) for the same material.
<!-- id: physics12a2-rol-6 -->

Explanation:

**A410.** (a) R = V/I gives 10 Ω, 11 Ω (11.1), 12.5 Ω; (b) the component is non-ohmic because its resistance is not constant (I is not proportional to V; the I–V graph curves rather than being a straight line through the origin); resistance increases as current increases, consistent with heating.
<!-- id: physics12a2-rol-7 -->

Explanation:

**A411.** (a) R = V/I = 230 / 8.0 ≈ 29 Ω (28.75); (b) P = VI = 230 × 8.0 = 1840 W; (c) E = Pt = 1840 × 1800 ≈ 3.3 × 10⁶ J.
<!-- id: physics12a2-rol-8 -->

Explanation:

**A412.** D. The lamp is non-ohmic: its resistance increases as the filament heats up
<!-- id: vce-physics12-ro-c1 -->

Explanation: R = V/I rises along the curve. Hotter metal has more vibrating ions, which impede electron flow.

#### Series and parallel circuits

**A413.** A. 0.40 A
<!-- id: physics12a2-spc-1 -->

Explanation: R total = 10 + 20 = 30 Ω; I = V/R = 12 / 30 = 0.40 A. 0.60 A would result from using only the 20 Ω resistor.

**A414.** A. 4.0 Ω
<!-- id: physics12a2-spc-2 -->

Explanation: 1/R = 1/6 + 1/12 = 3/12, so R = 4.0 Ω. The equivalent resistance of a parallel combination is always smaller than the smallest individual resistor.

**A415.** B. 12 V
<!-- id: physics12a2-spc-3 -->

Explanation: Branches of a parallel circuit are connected across the same two points, so each has the full supply potential difference of 12 V. In series, the supply voltage would be shared.

**A416.** C. in series with each other
<!-- id: physics12a2-spc-4 -->

Explanation: In series there is only one path for current, so an open circuit at any one lamp stops the current everywhere. In parallel the other branches would still carry current.

**A417.** D. total resistance decreases and supply current increases
<!-- id: physics12a2-spc-5 -->

Explanation: An extra parallel path offers more routes for current, so the equivalent resistance falls. With the supply voltage fixed, I = V/R increases.

**A418.** (a) parallel part: 1/R = 1/6 + 1/3 = 1/2, so 2.0 Ω; total = 4.0 + 2.0 = 6.0 Ω; (b) I = 12 / 6.0 = 2.0 A; (c) V = 2.0 × 4.0 = 8.0 V; (d) pd across the parallel part = 12 − 8.0 = 4.0 V, so I = 4.0 / 6.0 ≈ 0.67 A.
<!-- id: physics12a2-spc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A419.** Total R = 300 Ω; I = 9.0 / 300 = 0.030 A (30 mA); V₁ = 0.030 × 100 = 3.0 V; V₂ = 0.030 × 200 = 6.0 V; the voltages add to 9.0 V.
<!-- id: physics12a2-spc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A420.** In parallel each appliance is connected across the full supply voltage so it operates at its rated voltage; each appliance can be switched on or off independently without affecting the others; if one appliance fails (open circuit) the others continue to work, whereas in series the voltage would be shared and one failure would stop all of them.
<!-- id: physics12a2-spc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A421.** 2.0 A; about 1.3 A
<!-- id: vce-physics12-sp-c1 -->

Explanation: Parallel: (6 × 3) ÷ (6 + 3) = 2.0 Ω. Total 6.0 Ω, so I = 12 ÷ 6.0 = 2.0 A. V(parallel) = 2.0 × 2.0 = 4.0 V; I(3 Ω) = 4.0 ÷ 3.0 ≈ 1.3 A.

#### Electrical power and energy

**A422.** B. 1.8 × 10³ W
<!-- id: physics12a3-epe-1 -->

Explanation: P = VI = 230 × 8.0 = 1840 W ≈ 1.8 × 10³ W. The value 29 W comes from dividing V by I, which gives resistance (in Ω), not power.

**A423.** A. 3.6 × 10⁵ J
<!-- id: physics12a3-epe-2 -->

Explanation: E = Pt = 1200 W × (5.0 × 60 s) = 1200 × 300 = 3.6 × 10⁵ J. Using 5.0 for the time without converting minutes to seconds gives 6.0 × 10³ J, which is wrong.

**A424.** D. It becomes four times larger.
<!-- id: physics12a3-epe-3 -->

Explanation: P = I²R and R is fixed, so doubling I multiplies P by 2² = 4. Power is not proportional to current here, because the voltage across the resistor also doubles (V = IR), so P = VI increases fourfold.

**A425.** B. 2.0 Ω
<!-- id: physics12a3-epe-4 -->

Explanation: 1/R = 1/6 + 1/3 = 3/6, so R = 2.0 Ω. In parallel the equivalent resistance is always smaller than the smallest branch resistance; 9.0 Ω is the series value.

**A426.** A. $1.80
<!-- id: physics12a3-epe-5 -->

Explanation: Energy = 2.0 kW × 3.0 h = 6.0 kWh. Cost = 6.0 × 30 c = 180 c = $1.80.

**A427.** (a) R total = 4.0 + 8.0 = 12 Ω, so I = V/R = 12/12 = 1.0 A; (b) V = IR = 1.0 × 8.0 = 8.0 V; (c) P = VI = 8.0 × 1.0 = 8.0 W (equivalently I²R = 1.0² × 8.0 = 8.0 W).
<!-- id: physics12a3-epe-6 -->

Explanation:

**A428.** (a) I₁ = 240/60 = 4.0 A and I₂ = 240/120 = 2.0 A (same voltage across each branch); (b) total current = 4.0 + 2.0 = 6.0 A (equivalent resistance 40 Ω, 240/40 = 6.0 A); (c) P = VI = 240 × 6.0 = 1440 W (960 W + 480 W).
<!-- id: physics12a3-epe-7 -->

Explanation:

**A429.** (a) Efficiency = useful output / input = 350/500 = 0.70 = 70%; (b) waste power = 500 − 350 = 150 W; energy = Pt = 150 × (10 × 60) = 150 × 600 = 9.0 × 10⁴ J.
<!-- id: physics12a3-epe-8 -->

Explanation:

**A430.** D. $54
<!-- id: vce-physics12-pe-c1 -->

Explanation: Energy = 2.0 kW × 3 h × 30 = 180 kWh. Cost = 180 × $0.30 = $54.

#### Electrical safety

**A431.** B. It provides a low-resistance path to earth so that a fault current becomes large enough to blow the fuse or trip the circuit breaker.
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-1 -->

Explanation: If the live wire touches the casing, the earth wire gives a low-resistance path so a large current flows, which blows the fuse or trips the breaker and removes the shock hazard. The neutral wire, not the earth, carries the normal return current.

**A432.** C. 10 A
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-2 -->

Explanation: Normal current I = P/V = 1150/230 = 5.0 A. The fuse must be above this to avoid blowing in normal use (so not 2 A or 5 A) but not so high that it fails to protect the wiring, so 10 A is best. A 30 A fuse would allow dangerous currents before blowing.

**A433.** D. a difference between the current in the live and neutral wires of a few tens of milliamperes.
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-3 -->

Explanation: An RCD compares live and neutral currents. A difference (typically about 30 mA) means current is leaking to earth, possibly through a person, so it trips within milliseconds. It does not respond to high load current like a circuit breaker.

**A434.** B. Its casing and all user-accessible parts are non-conducting, so the live wire cannot make them live even if an internal fault occurs.
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-4 -->

Explanation: Double insulation (a second insulating barrier or a plastic casing) means exposed parts cannot become live, so no earth path is needed. The voltage supplied is still 230 V.

**A435.** B. The danger depends on the size of the current through the body, the path it takes and how long it flows.
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-5 -->

Explanation: Harm is caused by current; its magnitude, path (for example through the heart) and duration determine the effect. Voltage matters only because it drives current through the body's resistance, which depends on skin condition.

**A436.** The fuse contains a thin wire that heats (I²R) and melts when the current exceeds its rating, breaking the circuit; this stops excessive current that could overheat wiring or start a fire; it is placed in the live wire so that when it blows the appliance is disconnected from the live (high potential) side, so the appliance does not remain at a dangerous potential.
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-6 -->

Explanation:

**A437.** (a) Earthed: a large current flows through the low-resistance earth wire, which blows the fuse or trips the circuit breaker/RCD, disconnecting the supply so the casing is no longer live; (b) not earthed: the casing becomes live at about 230 V but no large current flows, so the fuse does not blow; a person touching the casing then provides a path to earth and receives a (possibly lethal) shock.
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-7 -->

Explanation:

**A438.** (a) I = V/R = 230/1500 = 0.15 A (153 mA); (b) this leakage current (about 150 mA) is well above 30 mA, so the RCD detects the live-neutral imbalance and cuts the supply within milliseconds, before serious harm; the 15 A breaker only trips on current above 15 A, and 0.15 A is far below this, so it would not trip even though 0.15 A is dangerous to a person.
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-8 -->

Explanation:

**A439.** C. Detecting a difference between the currents in the active and neutral wires, then cutting the power
<!-- id: vce-physics12-es-c1 -->

Explanation: A difference means current is leaking, possibly through a person. Fuses and circuit breakers protect wiring from overcurrent instead.

### Unit 2: Motion

#### Motion graphs

**A440.** C. velocity.
<!-- id: physics12a3-mg-1 -->

Explanation: Gradient = change in position / change in time = velocity. Acceleration is the gradient of a velocity-time graph.

**A441.** D. the displacement during the interval.
<!-- id: physics12a3-mg-2 -->

Explanation: Area under a v-t graph = ∫v dt = displacement (areas below the axis count as negative). The gradient gives acceleration.

**A442.** D. 250 m
<!-- id: physics12a3-mg-3 -->

Explanation: First stage: area of triangle = ½ × 5.0 × 20 = 50 m. Second stage: rectangle = 20 × 10 = 200 m. Total = 250 m. 300 m would result from assuming 20 m s⁻¹ for the whole 15 s.

**A443.** C. accelerating in the positive direction.
<!-- id: physics12a3-mg-4 -->

Explanation: An increasing positive gradient means velocity is positive and increasing, so the object accelerates in the positive direction. Constant velocity would give a straight line.

**A444.** B. 18 m
<!-- id: physics12a3-mg-5 -->

Explanation: The velocity reaches zero at 3.0 s. Each triangle has area ½ × 3.0 × 6.0 = 9.0 m, so distance = 9.0 + 9.0 = 18 m. The displacement is 0 m because the areas cancel, which is a common confusion with distance.

**A445.** (a) Section 1: a = 12/4.0 = +3.0 m s⁻²; section 2: a = 0; section 3: a = (0 − 12)/4.0 = −3.0 m s⁻²; (b) displacement = area = ½ × 4.0 × 12 + 6.0 × 12 + ½ × 4.0 × 12 = 24 + 72 + 24 = 120 m; average velocity = 120/14 = 8.6 m s⁻¹ (8.57).
<!-- id: physics12a3-mg-6 -->

Explanation:

**A446.** (a) 0–8 s: v = 40/8.0 = +5.0 m s⁻¹; 8–12 s: v = 0; 12–20 s: v = −40/8.0 = −5.0 m s⁻¹; (b) displacement = 0 m (returns to the start); distance = 40 + 40 = 80 m.
<!-- id: physics12a3-mg-7 -->

Explanation:

**A447.** The graph is a single straight line with a negative gradient (−9.8 m s⁻²) starting at a positive initial velocity; gradient = acceleration, which is constant and equal to −g throughout (including at the top); the intercept on the time axis is the instant at the top of the flight where v = 0; the area above the axis (upward displacement) equals the area below (downward displacement) because the ball returns to its launch height, so net displacement is zero.
<!-- id: physics12a3-mg-8 -->

Explanation:

**A448.** C. 80 m
<!-- id: vce-physics12-mg-c1 -->

Explanation: Area under the v–t graph: triangle ½ × 4.0 × 10 = 20 m, plus rectangle 6.0 × 10 = 60 m.

#### Equations of constant acceleration

**A449.** D. 20 m s⁻¹
<!-- id: physics12a3-eca-1 -->

Explanation: v = u + at = 0 + 2.5 × 8.0 = 20 m s⁻¹. 3.2 m s⁻¹ results from dividing instead of multiplying.

**A450.** A. 62.5 m
<!-- id: physics12a3-eca-2 -->

Explanation: v² = u² + 2as, 0 = 625 + 2(−5.0)s, so s = 625/10 = 62.5 m. 125 m is 25 × 5.0, which has no physical meaning here.

**A451.** B. 19.6 m
<!-- id: physics12a3-eca-3 -->

Explanation: s = ut + ½at² = 0 + ½ × 9.8 × 2.0² = 19.6 m. 39 m results from forgetting the ½ factor; 9.8 m is the distance in the first 1.0 s × 2.

**A452.** D. v² = u² + 2as
<!-- id: physics12a3-eca-4 -->

Explanation: v² = u² + 2as has no t. The other three all involve t, which is unknown here.

**A453.** B. 3.0 s
<!-- id: physics12a3-eca-5 -->

Explanation: Time to the top: t = u/g = 14.7/9.8 = 1.5 s. By symmetry the return takes another 1.5 s, so the total is 3.0 s. The 1.5 s is only the upward half.

**A454.** (a) s = ut + ½at²: 400 = 12 × 20 + ½ × a × 20², so 400 = 240 + 200a and a = 0.80 m s⁻²; (b) v = u + at = 12 + 0.80 × 20 = 28 m s⁻¹ (check: average speed ½(12 + 28) = 20 m s⁻¹, 20 × 20 = 400 m).
<!-- id: physics12a3-eca-6 -->

Explanation:

**A455.** Reaction distance = 20 × 0.60 = 12 m; braking distance from v² = u² + 2as: 0 = 400 − 2(8.0)s, so s = 400/16 = 25 m; total stopping distance = 12 + 25 = 37 m.
<!-- id: physics12a3-eca-7 -->

Explanation:

**A456.** (a) s = ½gt² = ½ × 9.8 × 3.0² = 44.1 m ≈ 44 m; (b) v = gt = 9.8 × 3.0 = 29.4 m s⁻¹ ≈ 29 m s⁻¹ (check: v² = 2gs = 2 × 9.8 × 44.1 = 864, v = 29.4).
<!-- id: physics12a3-eca-8 -->

Explanation:

**A457.** 82.5 m
<!-- id: vce-physics12-ca-c1 -->

Explanation: Reaction distance: 25 × 0.80 = 20 m. Braking distance: v² = u² + 2as gives 0 = 625 − 10s, so s = 62.5 m. Total 82.5 m.

#### Newton's laws of motion

**A458.** B. 4.0 m s⁻²
<!-- id: physics12a3-nlm-1 -->

Explanation: a = F_net/m = 12/3.0 = 4.0 m s⁻². 0.25 m s⁻² results from inverting the ratio.

**A459.** B. The gravitational force of the book on Earth.
<!-- id: physics12a3-nlm-2 -->

Explanation: Third-law pairs act on different objects, are the same type of force and are equal and opposite: Earth pulls the book down, so the book pulls Earth up. The normal force just balances weight (a first-law situation), but it is not the third-law partner because both act on the book.

**A460.** A. 678 N
<!-- id: physics12a3-nlm-3 -->

Explanation: N − mg = ma, so N = m(g + a) = 60 × (9.8 + 1.5) = 678 N. 588 N is the weight at rest; 498 N would be for a downward acceleration.

**A461.** D. 20 N
<!-- id: physics12a3-nlm-4 -->

Explanation: Constant velocity means zero acceleration, so by Newton's first law the net force is zero. The friction force must balance the 20 N applied force.

**A462.** D. 4.9 m s⁻²
<!-- id: physics12a3-nlm-5 -->

Explanation: The component of weight along the slope is mg sin 30° = 0.5mg, so a = g sin 30° = 4.9 m s⁻². 8.5 m s⁻² would be g cos 30°, the perpendicular component.

**A463.** (a) Treat as one system: a = F/m total = 10/(2.0 + 3.0) = 2.0 m s⁻²; (b) the net force on the 3.0 kg block is the contact force: F = ma = 3.0 × 2.0 = 6.0 N.
<!-- id: physics12a3-nlm-6 -->

Explanation:

**A464.** (a) F_net = 3000 − 1000 = 2000 N, a = 2000/1200 = 1.67 m s⁻²; (b) v = u + at = 0 + 1.67 × 10 = 16.7 m s⁻¹; (c) s = ½at² = ½ × 1.67 × 10² = 83 m.
<!-- id: physics12a3-nlm-7 -->

Explanation:

**A465.** Newton's first law: with no net force acting on it, the passenger keeps moving at the car's original velocity while the car slows down, so appears to lurch forward; a seatbelt exerts a backward force on the passenger, so the passenger decelerates with the car (F = ma) and does not hit the dashboard or windscreen; the belt also spreads the force over a larger area/time, reducing injury.
<!-- id: physics12a3-nlm-8 -->

Explanation:

**A466.** D. 708 N
<!-- id: vce-physics12-nl-c1 -->

Explanation: N − mg = ma, so N = 60 × (9.8 + 2.0) = 708 N. Accelerating down would give 468 N.

#### Momentum and impulse

**A467.** A. 6.0 kg m s⁻¹
<!-- id: physics12a3-mi-1 -->

Explanation: p = mv = 0.50 × 12 = 6.0 kg m s⁻¹. 3.0 kg m s⁻¹ would be ½mv without squaring, a mix-up with kinetic energy ideas.

**A468.** D. 2.0 m s⁻¹
<!-- id: physics12a3-mi-2 -->

Explanation: Momentum is conserved: 2.0 × 3.0 = (2.0 + 1.0)v, so v = 6.0/3.0 = 2.0 m s⁻¹.

**A469.** D. 5.25 N s
<!-- id: physics12a3-mi-3 -->

Explanation: Taking the rebound direction as positive, Δp = 0.15 × (15 − (−20)) = 0.15 × 35 = 5.25 N s. 0.75 N s results from subtracting the speeds (20 − 15) and ignoring the direction reversal.

**A470.** D. They increase the time over which the car stops, reducing the average force for the same change in momentum.
<!-- id: physics12a3-mi-4 -->

Explanation: Impulse FΔt = Δp is fixed by the change in momentum; a longer collision time Δt gives a smaller average force F. The impulse is not reduced, and a crumpling collision is inelastic.

**A471.** A. inelastic, because momentum is conserved but kinetic energy is not.
<!-- id: physics12a3-mi-5 -->

Explanation: Momentum before = 4.0 and after = 1.0 + 3.0 = 4.0 kg m s⁻¹, so it is conserved. Kinetic energy before = ½(1.0)(4.0²) = 8.0 J; after = 0.5 + 4.5 = 5.0 J, so kinetic energy is lost and the collision is inelastic.

**A472.** (a) Taking the final direction as positive: Δp = 0.16 × (40 − (−30)) = 0.16 × 70 = 11.2 N s; (b) F = Δp/Δt = 11.2/(5.0 × 10⁻³) = 2.2 × 10³ N (2240 N).
<!-- id: physics12a3-mi-6 -->

Explanation:

**A473.** (a) 1000 × 15 = 3000 v, so v = 5.0 m s⁻¹; (b) KE before = ½ × 1000 × 15² = 1.125 × 10⁵ J; KE after = ½ × 3000 × 5.0² = 3.75 × 10⁴ J; loss = 7.5 × 10⁴ J; (c) inelastic, because kinetic energy is not conserved (momentum is conserved).
<!-- id: physics12a3-mi-7 -->

Explanation:

**A474.** (a) Total momentum is zero: 60v + 40 × 3.0 = 0, so v = −2.0 m s⁻¹ (2.0 m s⁻¹ in the opposite direction); (b) KE = ½ × 40 × 3.0² + ½ × 60 × 2.0² = 180 + 120 = 300 J; this came from chemical energy stored in the skaters' muscles (momentum is conserved but kinetic energy increases).
<!-- id: physics12a3-mi-8 -->

Explanation:

**A475.** 7.5 N s; 750 N
<!-- id: vce-physics12-mi-c1 -->

Explanation: Taking the return direction as positive: Δp = 0.15 × (30 − (−20)) = 7.5 N s. F = Δp/Δt = 7.5 ÷ 0.010 = 750 N. Because the ball reverses, the speeds add: the change in velocity is 50 m/s, not 10 m/s.

#### Work, energy and power

**A476.** C. 100 J
<!-- id: physics12a3-wep-1 -->

Explanation: W = Fs cos θ = 50 × 4.0 × cos 60° = 50 × 4.0 × 0.5 = 100 J. 173 J would come from using sin 60° and 200 J ignores the angle.

**A477.** A. 2.0 × 10⁵ J
<!-- id: physics12a3-wep-2 -->

Explanation: KE = ½mv² = ½ × 1000 × 20² = 2.0 × 10⁵ J. 4.0 × 10⁵ J omits the ½.

**A478.** B. increases by a factor of four.
<!-- id: physics12a3-wep-3 -->

Explanation: KE ∝ v², so doubling v multiplies KE by 2² = 4.

**A479.** B. 9.9 m s⁻¹
<!-- id: physics12a3-wep-4 -->

Explanation: mgh = ½mv², so v = √(2gh) = √(2 × 9.8 × 5.0) = √98 = 9.9 m s⁻¹. The mass cancels, so 0.20 kg is irrelevant.

**A480.** B. 2.0 kW
<!-- id: physics12a3-wep-5 -->

Explanation: At constant speed the lifting force equals the weight: F = mg = 500 × 9.8 = 4900 N. P = Fv = 4900 × 0.40 = 1960 W ≈ 2.0 kW. 200 W is mv with no g.

**A481.** (a) E = ½kx² = ½ × 200 × 0.10² = 1.0 J; (b) all the elastic energy becomes kinetic: ½mv² = 1.0, so v = √(2 × 1.0/0.050) = √40 = 6.3 m s⁻¹.
<!-- id: physics12a3-wep-6 -->

Explanation:

**A482.** (a) Loss of gravitational PE = mgh = 2.0 × 9.8 × 3.0 = 58.8 J; KE at bottom = ½ × 2.0 × 6.0² = 36 J; energy lost = 58.8 − 36 = 22.8 J ≈ 23 J; (b) work done by friction = Fs, so F = 22.8/5.0 = 4.6 N.
<!-- id: physics12a3-wep-7 -->

Explanation:

**A483.** (a) ΔE = mgh = 70 × 9.8 × 12 = 8232 J ≈ 8.2 × 10³ J; (b) P = E/t = 8232/30 = 274 W ≈ 2.7 × 10² W; (c) chemical energy = useful energy/efficiency = 8232/0.25 = 32 928 J ≈ 3.3 × 10⁴ J.
<!-- id: physics12a3-wep-8 -->

Explanation:

**A484.** B. 510 W
<!-- id: vce-physics12-we-c1 -->

Explanation: Work = mgh = 70 × 9.8 × 3.0 ≈ 2058 J; P = 2058 ÷ 4.0 ≈ 510 W.

#### Torque and equilibrium

**A485.** B. 12 N m
<!-- id: physics12a3-te-1 -->

Explanation: τ = rF sin θ = 0.30 × 40 × sin 90° = 12 N m. 133 comes from dividing F by r.

**A486.** B. The net force and the net torque must both be zero.
<!-- id: physics12a3-te-2 -->

Explanation: Zero net force prevents translational acceleration and zero net torque prevents rotational acceleration. A pure couple gives zero net force but still rotates the object.

**A487.** C. 1.5 m
<!-- id: physics12a3-te-3 -->

Explanation: Balance torques about the pivot: 30g × 2.0 = 40g × d, so d = 60/40 = 1.5 m. The heavier child sits closer to the pivot.

**A488.** D. 7.5 N m
<!-- id: physics12a3-te-4 -->

Explanation: τ = rF sin θ = 0.25 × 60 × sin 30° = 0.25 × 60 × 0.5 = 7.5 N m. Using cos 30° would give 13 N m, but only the component perpendicular to the spanner produces torque.

**A489.** A. A longer spanner gives a larger lever arm, so more torque is produced by the same force.
<!-- id: physics12a3-te-5 -->

Explanation: τ = rF, so increasing r (the lever arm) increases torque for the same force F. The torque needed to turn the nut does not change.

**A490.** Take torques about A: B × 4.0 = (30 × 9.8) × 1.0 + (20 × 9.8) × 2.0 = 294 + 392 = 686 N m, so B = 171.5 N ≈ 1.7 × 10² N; vertical forces balance: A + B = (30 + 20) × 9.8 = 490 N, so A = 490 − 171.5 = 318.5 N ≈ 3.2 × 10² N.
<!-- id: physics12a3-te-6 -->

Explanation:

**A491.** (a) Torques about the hinge: T × 2.0 = (10 × 9.8) × 1.0 + (4.0 × 9.8) × 2.0 = 98 + 78.4 = 176.4, so T = 88.2 N ≈ 88 N; (b) vertical forces: hinge force + T = total weight = 14 × 9.8 = 137.2 N, so hinge force = 137.2 − 88.2 = 49 N upward.
<!-- id: physics12a3-te-7 -->

Explanation:

**A492.** While tilting, the weight acts at the centre of mass and produces a torque about the pivot edge; the cupboard returns to its original position while the vertical line through the centre of mass still falls inside the base (restoring torque); it topples when this line passes beyond the pivot edge; a tall, narrow cupboard has a high centre of mass and a narrow base, so this happens at a smaller tilt angle than for a short, wide one.
<!-- id: physics12a3-te-8 -->

Explanation:

**A493.** D. 1.5 m from the pivot
<!-- id: vce-physics12-te-c1 -->

Explanation: Equilibrium: clockwise torque = anticlockwise torque. 30g × 2.0 = 40g × d, so d = 1.5 m.

### Challenge questions

#### Challenge set

**A494.** A. 4.5 × 10⁵ J
<!-- id: vce-physics12-challenge-1 -->

Explanation: Q = mL = 0.20 × 2.26 × 10⁶ ≈ 4.5 × 10⁵ J. The temperature does not change during boiling.

**A495.** D. X is about three times hotter than Y
<!-- id: vce-physics12-challenge-2 -->

Explanation: By Wien's law, T is inversely proportional to λ(max): 900 ÷ 300 = 3.

**A496.** B. 2.0 × 10⁸ m/s
<!-- id: vce-physics12-challenge-3 -->

Explanation: v = c/n = 3.0 × 10⁸ ÷ 1.50 = 2.0 × 10⁸ m/s.

**A497.** A. 7
<!-- id: vce-physics12-challenge-4 -->

Explanation: (1/2)⁶ ≈ 1.6% and (1/2)⁷ ≈ 0.78%, so 7 half-lives.

**A498.** D. Increases the total current, and each bulb stays equally bright
<!-- id: vce-physics12-challenge-5 -->

Explanation: Each bulb still has the full battery voltage across it. More parallel paths lower the total resistance, so the total current rises.

**A499.** A. 3.6 × 10⁶ J
<!-- id: vce-physics12-challenge-6 -->

Explanation: 1 kW × 1 h = 1000 W × 3600 s = 3.6 × 10⁶ J.

**A500.** 15 m/s; 37.5 m
<!-- id: vce-physics12-challenge-7 -->

Explanation: v = u + at = 0 + 3.0 × 5.0 = 15 m/s. s = ut + ½at² = ½ × 3.0 × 25 = 37.5 m.

**A501.** B. 4.0 m/s in the opposite direction
<!-- id: vce-physics12-challenge-8 -->

Explanation: Total momentum stays zero: 2.0 × 6.0 = 3.0 × v, so v = 4.0 m/s the other way.

**A502.** About 6.5 × 10³ J
<!-- id: vce-physics12-challenge-9 -->

Explanation: Useful energy = mgh = 50 × 9.8 × 10 = 4900 J. Input = 4900 ÷ 0.75 ≈ 6.5 × 10³ J.

**A503.** C. 3.0 N m
<!-- id: vce-physics12-challenge-10 -->

Explanation: τ = rF sin θ = 0.30 × 20 × sin 30° = 3.0 N m. Only the perpendicular component produces turning.

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**A504.** An electric kettle
<!-- id: vce-physics12-x1 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** I = 8.7 A; R = 26 Ω (26.45 Ω) *(2 marks)*

1. I = P ÷ V = 2000 ÷ 230 = 8.7 A. *(1 mark)*
2. R = V² ÷ P = 230² ÷ 2000 = 26.45 Ω ≈ 26 Ω (or R = V ÷ I with the unrounded current). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using R = P ÷ V, which gives the current, not the resistance. Use R = V ÷ I or R = V² ÷ P.

Topic: Resistance and Ohm's law (vce-physics12-resistance-and-ohms-law)

**b.** 5.04 × 10⁵ J *(2 marks)*

1. Q = mcΔT, with ΔT = 100.0 − 20.0 = 80.0 K. *(1 mark)*
2. Q = 1.50 × 4200 × 80.0 = 5.04 × 10⁵ J. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using the final temperature (100 °C) as ΔT. ΔT is the change in temperature, 80.0 K.

Topic: Specific heat capacity (vce-physics12-specific-heat-capacity)

**c.** 252 s *(2 marks)*

1. Energy = power × time, so t = E ÷ P. *(1 mark)*
2. t = 5.04 × 10⁵ ÷ 2000 = 252 s (about 4.2 minutes). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Dividing the power by the energy. Rearrange E = Pt to t = E ÷ P.

Topic: Electrical power and energy (vce-physics12-electrical-power-and-energy)

**d.i.** 2.26 × 10⁵ J *(1 mark)*

1. Q = mL = 0.100 × 2.26 × 10⁶ = 2.26 × 10⁵ J. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using Q = mcΔT. While water boils its temperature does not change, so use the latent heat, Q = mL.

Topic: Latent heat and phase change (vce-physics12-latent-heat-and-phase-change)

**d.ii.** The energy is used to overcome the attractions between water particles (increasing their potential energy) as they change to steam, not to increase their kinetic energy, so the temperature stays constant. *(1 mark)*

1. During a change of state, the energy supplied breaks the bonds between particles (increasing their potential energy) rather than increasing their average kinetic energy, so the temperature does not rise. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the energy is 'lost'. It is used to separate the particles during the change of state.

Topic: Latent heat and phase change (vce-physics12-latent-heat-and-phase-change)

**e.** Convection *(1 mark)*

1. Water heated by the element expands, becomes less dense and rises, while cooler water sinks: convection currents carry heat through the water. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming conduction. Water is a poor conductor; in a liquid, heat is mostly spread by convection currents.

Topic: Conduction, convection and radiation (vce-physics12-conduction-convection-and-radiation)

**f.** The earth wire gives a low-resistance path from the case to the ground, so a large current flows. This blows the fuse or trips the circuit breaker, disconnecting the live wire so the case does not stay live. *(2 marks)*

1. If the live wire touches the case, the earth wire provides a low-resistance path to the ground, so a very large current flows through it rather than through the user. *(1 mark)*
2. The large current blows the fuse or trips the circuit breaker, which disconnects the supply. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the earth wire 'absorbs' the electricity. It works by allowing a large current that triggers the fuse or circuit breaker.

Topic: Electrical safety (vce-physics12-electrical-safety)

**A505.** A cyclist on the road
<!-- id: vce-physics12-x2 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** 2.0 m s⁻² *(1 mark)*

1. a = (v − u) ÷ t = (10 − 0) ÷ 5.0 = 2.0 m s⁻². *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Dividing the time by the change in speed. Acceleration is the change in velocity per second.

Topic: Equations of constant acceleration (vce-physics12-equations-of-constant-acceleration)

**b.** 25 m *(2 marks)*

1. For uniform acceleration, s = ½(u + v)t. *(1 mark)*
2. s = ½ × (0 + 10) × 5.0 = 25 m. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using s = vt with v = 10 m s⁻¹, which gives 50 m. The speed was only 10 m s⁻¹ at the end, so use the average speed.

Topic: Equations of constant acceleration (vce-physics12-equations-of-constant-acceleration)

**c.** 200 N *(2 marks)*

1. Net force = ma = 80 × 2.0 = 160 N. *(1 mark)*
2. Net force = driving force − resistance, so driving force = 160 + 40 = 200 N. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving the net force (160 N) as the driving force. The driving force must also overcome the 40 N of resistance.

Topic: Newton's laws of motion (vce-physics12-newtons-laws-of-motion)

**d.** 4.0 × 10³ J *(1 mark)*

1. KE = ½mv² = ½ × 80 × 10² = 4.0 × 10³ J. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Forgetting to square the speed. KE = ½mv², so the speed is squared.

Topic: Work, energy and power (vce-physics12-work-energy-and-power)

**e.** 14 m s⁻¹ *(2 marks)*

1. Energy is conserved: ½mv² = ½mu² + mgh, so v² = u² + 2gh = 10² + 2 × 9.8 × 5.0 = 198. *(1 mark)*
2. v = √198 = 14 m s⁻¹ (14.1 m s⁻¹). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Ignoring the starting kinetic energy, which gives √98 ≈ 9.9 m s⁻¹. The cyclist was already moving at the top.

Topic: Work, energy and power (vce-physics12-work-energy-and-power)

**f.** Impulse 1.1 × 10³ N s (1120 N s); average force 560 N *(2 marks)*

1. Impulse = change in momentum = m(v − u) = 80 × (0 − 14) = −1120 N s, so its magnitude is 1.1 × 10³ N s. *(1 mark)*
2. Average force = impulse ÷ time = 1120 ÷ 2.0 = 560 N. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using the cyclist's weight instead of mass. Momentum is mass × velocity, with mass in kilograms.

Topic: Momentum and impulse (vce-physics12-momentum-and-impulse)

**g.** The cyclist's body keeps moving forward at the same velocity unless an unbalanced force acts on it. The brakes stop the bicycle, but nothing immediately stops the body, so it continues forward. *(1 mark)*

1. An object keeps moving with constant velocity unless an unbalanced force acts on it; the brakes act on the bicycle, not directly on the rider, so the rider's body continues forward. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying a force pushes the cyclist forward. No forward force is needed; the body simply keeps moving.

Topic: Newton's laws of motion (vce-physics12-newtons-laws-of-motion)

**A506.** Radiation in medicine
<!-- id: vce-physics12-x3 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** 50 MBq *(2 marks)*

1. 24 hours is 4 half-lives. *(1 mark)*
2. Activity = 800 ÷ 2⁴ = 50 MBq. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Dividing by 4 instead of by 2⁴.

Topic: Half-life and activity (vce-physics12-half-life-and-activity)

**b.** Gamma rays penetrate the body and reach detectors outside it while ionising weakly; a short half-life means the patient's exposure drops quickly. *(2 marks)*

1. Gamma radiation is penetrating, so it leaves the body and can be detected, and it is weakly ionising. *(1 mark)*
2. The short half-life means activity falls quickly, limiting the patient's dose. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Choosing an alpha emitter. Alpha would not leave the body and is highly ionising.

Topic: Alpha, beta and gamma radiation (vce-physics12-alpha-beta-and-gamma-radiation)

**c.** Alpha *(1 mark)*

1. Alpha particles are the most ionising and are stopped by paper or skin. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Choosing gamma. Gamma is the most penetrating and least ionising.

Topic: Alpha, beta and gamma radiation (vce-physics12-alpha-beta-and-gamma-radiation)

**d.** 7.1 × 10⁻⁴ Gy; 7.1 × 10⁻⁴ Sv *(2 marks)*

1. Absorbed dose = energy ÷ mass = 0.050 ÷ 70 = 7.1 × 10⁻⁴ Gy. *(1 mark)*
2. Equivalent dose = absorbed dose × weighting factor = 7.1 × 10⁻⁴ Sv. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Multiplying by the mass instead of dividing.

Topic: Radiation dose and biological effects (vce-physics12-radiation-dose-and-biological-effects)

**e.** ⁶⁰₂₇Co → ⁶⁰₂₈Ni + ⁰₋₁e + antineutrino *(2 marks)*

1. Mass number unchanged (60); atomic number increases by 1 (27 → 28). *(1 mark)*
2. ⁶⁰₂₇Co → ⁶⁰₂₈Ni + ⁰₋₁e + antineutrino. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Decreasing the atomic number. In beta-minus decay a neutron becomes a proton, so it increases.

Topic: Nuclear stability and isotopes (vce-physics12-nuclear-stability-and-isotopes)

**f.** 3.2 × 10⁻¹¹ J *(1 mark)*

1. 200 × 10⁶ eV × 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ J/eV = 3.2 × 10⁻¹¹ J. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Forgetting the 'mega' (10⁶).

Topic: Nuclear fission and fusion (vce-physics12-nuclear-fission-and-fusion)

**A507.** Light bending and splitting
<!-- id: vce-physics12-x4 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** 25° *(2 marks)*

1. Snell's law: 1.00 × sin 40° = 1.50 × sin r. *(1 mark)*
2. sin r = 0.643 ÷ 1.50 = 0.429, so r = 25°. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Expecting the light to bend away from the normal. Entering a denser medium, it bends towards the normal.

Topic: Refraction and Snell's law (vce-physics12-refraction-and-snells-law)

**b.** 2.0 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹ *(1 mark)*

1. v = c ÷ n = 3.0 × 10⁸ ÷ 1.50 = 2.0 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Multiplying by the refractive index. Light slows down in glass.

Topic: Refraction and Snell's law (vce-physics12-refraction-and-snells-law)

**c.** 41.8° *(2 marks)*

1. At the critical angle the refracted angle is 90°: 1.50 × sin c = 1.00 × sin 90°. *(1 mark)*
2. sin c = 1 ÷ 1.50, so c = 41.8°. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using sin c = 1.50, which is impossible. Total internal reflection needs light going from higher to lower n.

Topic: Total internal reflection and dispersion (vce-physics12-total-internal-reflection-and-dispersion)

**d.** The core has a higher refractive index than the cladding. Light strikes the core–cladding boundary at angles greater than the critical angle, so it is totally internally reflected along the fibre. *(2 marks)*

1. The fibre's core has a higher refractive index than the surrounding cladding. *(1 mark)*
2. Light meets the boundary at more than the critical angle, so it reflects completely and stays inside the core. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the light reflects off a mirror coating. Fibres rely on total internal reflection at the boundary.

Topic: Total internal reflection and dispersion (vce-physics12-total-internal-reflection-and-dispersion)

**e.** The refractive index of glass is slightly different for each wavelength, so each colour refracts by a different amount; violet refracts most and red least. *(2 marks)*

1. Glass has a slightly different refractive index for different wavelengths (dispersion). *(1 mark)*
2. Each colour is refracted by a different angle, so they separate, with violet bent most and red least. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the prism adds colour to the light. The colours are already in white light.

Topic: Total internal reflection and dispersion (vce-physics12-total-internal-reflection-and-dispersion)

**f.** 3.0 m *(1 mark)*

1. λ = c ÷ f = 3.0 × 10⁸ ÷ 1.00 × 10⁸ = 3.0 m. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Treating MHz as 10³ Hz. Mega means 10⁶.

Topic: The electromagnetic spectrum (vce-physics12-the-electromagnetic-spectrum)

**A508.** Challenge: a kettle, a fuse and the power bill
<!-- id: vce-physics12-cx1 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** 10 A; 24 Ω *(2 marks)*

1. I = P/V = 2400 ÷ 240 = 10 A. *(1 mark)*
2. R = V/I = 240 ÷ 10 = 24 Ω. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using R = P/I.

Topic: Resistance and Ohm's law (vce-physics12-resistance-and-ohms-law)

**b.** About 233 s (3.9 minutes) *(3 marks)*

1. Q = mcΔT = 1.5 × 4200 × 80 = 504 000 J. *(1 mark)*
2. Useful power = 0.90 × 2400 = 2160 W. *(1 mark)*
3. t = 504 000 ÷ 2160 ≈ 233 s. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Forgetting the 90% efficiency.

Topic: Specific heat capacity (vce-physics12-specific-heat-capacity)

**c.** About 105 s *(2 marks)*

1. Q = mL = 0.10 × 2.26 × 10⁶ = 226 000 J. *(1 mark)*
2. t = 226 000 ÷ 2160 ≈ 105 s. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using mcΔT for boiling, where the temperature does not change.

Topic: Latent heat and phase change (vce-physics12-latent-heat-and-phase-change)

**d.** 13 A: it is just above the normal 10 A current, so it will not blow in normal use but will blow on a fault. *(2 marks)*

1. 13 A chosen. *(1 mark)*
2. Reason: above the operating current but as low as possible. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Choosing 30 A 'to be safe', which would let a dangerous current flow.

Topic: Electrical safety (vce-physics12-electrical-safety)

**e.** $8.64 *(2 marks)*

1. Time per day = 24 min = 0.40 h; energy = 2.4 kW × 0.40 h × 30 = 28.8 kWh. *(1 mark)*
2. Cost = 28.8 × $0.30 = $8.64. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Multiplying watts by minutes.

Topic: Electrical power and energy (vce-physics12-electrical-power-and-energy)

**A509.** Challenge: braking and collisions
<!-- id: vce-physics12-cx2 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** −5.0 m/s²; 6.0 × 10³ N *(2 marks)*

1. a = (0 − 20) ÷ 4.0 = −5.0 m/s². *(1 mark)*
2. F = ma = 1200 × 5.0 = 6.0 × 10³ N, opposing the motion. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving a positive acceleration with no direction.

Topic: Newton's laws of motion (vce-physics12-newtons-laws-of-motion)

**b.** 40 m; 55 m *(2 marks)*

1. Braking: average velocity 10 m/s × 4.0 s = 40 m. *(1 mark)*
2. Reaction: 20 × 0.75 = 15 m; total 55 m. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Including braking in the reaction distance.

Topic: Equations of constant acceleration (vce-physics12-equations-of-constant-acceleration)

**c.** KE = ½ × 1200 × 20² = 2.4 × 10⁵ J; W = Fd = 6.0 × 10³ × 40 = 2.4 × 10⁵ J. *(2 marks)*

1. KE = 2.4 × 10⁵ J. *(1 mark)*
2. W = Fd = 2.4 × 10⁵ J, which is equal. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using the total stopping distance, which includes the reaction distance.

Topic: Work, energy and power (vce-physics12-work-energy-and-power)

**d.** 6.0 m/s *(2 marks)*

1. Momentum before: 1200 × 10 = 12 000 kg m/s. *(1 mark)*
2. v = 12 000 ÷ 2000 = 6.0 m/s. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Dividing by 1200 kg instead of the combined mass.

Topic: Momentum and impulse (vce-physics12-momentum-and-impulse)

**e.** The change in momentum is fixed by the crash. A crumple zone increases the collision time, so the average force (F = Δp/Δt) is smaller. *(2 marks)*

1. Same change in momentum (impulse). *(1 mark)*
2. Longer time gives a smaller average force. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying crumple zones reduce the change in momentum.

Topic: Momentum and impulse (vce-physics12-momentum-and-impulse)

## VCE Units 3 & 4

### Gravitational fields

#### Newton's law of gravitation

**A510.** A. F/4
<!-- id: physics-1-1-1 -->

Explanation: F ∝ 1/r², so doubling r divides the force by 2² = 4.

**A511.** B. stays the same
<!-- id: physics-1-1-2 -->

Explanation: F ∝ m₁m₂/r²: the numerator increases by 4 and the denominator by 4, so F is unchanged.

**A512.** C. 687 N
<!-- id: physics-1-1-3 -->

Explanation: F = GMm/R² = 6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 5.97×10²⁴ × 70 / (6.37×10⁶)² ≈ 687 N.

**A513.** F = GMm/R² = 6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 6.42×10²³ × 2.0 / (3.39×10⁶)² ≈ 7.5 N.
<!-- id: physics-1-1-4 -->

Explanation: Use the radius of Mars as the separation, and remember to square it.

**A514.** D. 2.7 × 10⁻⁶ N
<!-- id: physics-1-1-5 -->

Explanation: F = Gm₁m₂/r² = 6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 10⁶ / 25 ≈ 2.7×10⁻⁶ N. Dividing by r instead of r² gives 1.3×10⁻⁵ N.

**A515.** A. equal in magnitude and opposite in direction
<!-- id: physics-1-1-6 -->

Explanation: Gravitational forces form a Newton's third law pair: equal magnitude, opposite direction, acting on different bodies.

**A516.** B. N m² kg⁻²
<!-- id: physics-1-1-7 -->

Explanation: Rearranging F = Gm₁m₂/r² gives G = Fr²/(m₁m₂), with units N m² kg⁻².

**A517.** The forces are equal in size, but a = F/m. Earth's mass is about 10²⁴ times larger than the ball's, so Earth's acceleration is far too small to notice, while the ball accelerates at about 9.8 m s⁻².
<!-- id: physics-1-1-8 -->

Explanation: Full marks need both the equal forces (third law) and a = F/m with Earth's huge mass.

**A518.** C. 1.98 × 10²⁰ N
<!-- id: physics-1-1-9 -->

Explanation: F = GMm/r² = 6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 5.97×10²⁴ × 7.35×10²² / (3.84×10⁸)² ≈ 1.98×10²⁰ N. Forgetting to square r gives 7.62×10²⁸ N.

**A519.** D. 1/9 of the original
<!-- id: physics-1-1-10 -->

Explanation: F ∝ 1/r², so tripling r reduces F by 3² = 9.

**A520.** A. doubles
<!-- id: physics-1-1-11 -->

Explanation: F ∝ m₁m₂/r²: the factor is (1/2)/(1/2)² = (1/2)/(1/4) = 2.

**A521.** F = GMm/r² = 6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 1.90×10²⁷ × 8.93×10²² / (4.22×10⁸)² ≈ 6.4 × 10²² N.
<!-- id: physics-1-1-12 -->

Explanation: A common error is forgetting to square the separation.

**A522.** B. 3.52 × 10²² N
<!-- id: physics-1-1-13 -->

Explanation: F = GMm/r² = 6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 1.99×10³⁰ × 5.97×10²⁴ / (1.50×10¹¹)² ≈ 3.52×10²² N.

**A523.** C. about 90% of the way from Earth to the Moon
<!-- id: physics-1-1-14 -->

Explanation: Setting GM_E/x² = GM_M/(d − x)² gives x/(d − x) = √81 = 9, so x = 0.9d.

**A524.** D. It is always attractive and acts along the line joining their centres
<!-- id: physics-1-1-15 -->

Explanation: Gravity is always attractive, acts along the line joining the centres of mass, and is not affected by intervening material.

**A525.** G is very small (6.67 × 10⁻¹¹), so the gravitational force between everyday objects is tiny. We feel a large force from Earth only because its mass is enormous (about 6 × 10²⁴ kg).
<!-- id: physics-1-1-16 -->

Explanation: Point to the small value of G and the very large mass of Earth.

**A526.** A. 3.68 × 10³ N
<!-- id: physics-1-1-17 -->

Explanation: The orbital radius is 2R, so F = GMm/(2R)² is one quarter of the surface weight: ≈ 14 700/4 ≈ 3.68×10³ N. Using r = R gives 1.47×10⁴ N; halving instead of quartering gives 7.36×10³ N.

**A527.** B. 2.0 × 10⁻⁹ N
<!-- id: physics-1-1-18 -->

Explanation: Doubling the separation reduces the force by a factor of 4: 8.0×10⁻⁹/4 = 2.0×10⁻⁹ N.

**A528.** C. the gravitational acceleration a = GM/r² does not depend on the mass of the falling object
<!-- id: physics-1-1-19 -->

Explanation: F = GMm/r² and a = F/m, so m cancels; with no air resistance both accelerate equally. The forces themselves differ.

**A529.** The claim is incorrect. The forces are a Newton's third law pair, and F = GMm/r² uses both masses symmetrically, so the forces are equal in size and opposite in direction. Earth's larger mass means it accelerates less, not that it pulls harder.
<!-- id: physics-1-1-20 -->

Explanation: Mention the third law, or the symmetry of the formula, and separate force from acceleration.

**A530.** D. 5.96 × 10²⁴ kg
<!-- id: physics-1-1-21 -->

Explanation: M = gR²/G = 9.8 × (6.37×10⁶)² / 6.67×10⁻¹¹ ≈ 5.96×10²⁴ kg. Forgetting to square R gives 9.36×10¹⁷ kg.

**A531.** A. a straight line through the origin
<!-- id: physics-1-1-22 -->

Explanation: F = (Gm₁m₂)(1/r²), so F is directly proportional to 1/r² with gradient Gm₁m₂.

**A532.** B. 6.7 × 10⁻⁹ N
<!-- id: physics-1-1-23 -->

Explanation: F = Gm₁m₂/r² = 6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 25 / 0.25 = 6.67×10⁻⁹ N. Dividing by r instead of r² gives 3.3×10⁻⁹ N.

**A533.** The radius increases by a factor of 4.2×10⁷/7.0×10⁶ = 6, so the force decreases by a factor of 6² = 36.
<!-- id: physics-1-1-24 -->

Explanation: Inverse square law: the force factor is the square of the radius ratio.

**A534.** r = √(Gm₁m₂/F) = √(6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 2500 / 1.0×10⁻⁸) = √16.7 ≈ 4.1 m.
<!-- id: physics-1-1-25 -->

Explanation: Rearrange F = Gm₁m₂/r² for r and remember the square root.

**A535.** A. 2/9 of the original
<!-- id: physics-1-1-c1 -->

Explanation: F ∝ m₁m₂/r²: doubling a mass gives × 2, tripling r gives × 1/9, so F becomes 2/9 of its value.

#### Gravitational field strength

**A536.** C. 9.81 N kg⁻¹
<!-- id: physics-1-2-1 -->

Explanation: g = 6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 5.97×10²⁴ / (6.37×10⁶)² ≈ 9.81 N kg⁻¹. Not squaring R gives 6.25×10⁷.

**A537.** D. m s⁻²
<!-- id: physics-1-2-2 -->

Explanation: 1 N = 1 kg m s⁻², so N kg⁻¹ = m s⁻², the unit of acceleration.

**A538.** A. 8.7 N kg⁻¹
<!-- id: physics-1-2-3 -->

Explanation: r = 6.37×10⁶ + 4.0×10⁵ = 6.77×10⁶ m, so g = GM/r² ≈ 8.69 N kg⁻¹, which is still about 89% of the surface value.

**A539.** g = GM/R² = 6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 1.90×10²⁷ / (6.99×10⁷)² ≈ 25.9 N kg⁻¹.
<!-- id: physics-1-2-4 -->

Explanation: About 2.6 times Earth's surface value.

**A540.** B. 2.45 N kg⁻¹
<!-- id: physics-1-2-5 -->

Explanation: The distance from the centre is 2R, so g = 9.8/2² ≈ 2.45 N kg⁻¹.

**A541.** C. 1.62 N kg⁻¹
<!-- id: physics-1-2-6 -->

Explanation: g = GM/R² = 6.67×10⁻¹¹ × 7.35×10²² / (1.74×10⁶)² ≈ 1.62 N kg⁻¹.

**A542.** D. half
<!-- id: physics-1-2-7 -->

Explanation: g ∝ M/R² = 2/2² = 1/2.

**A543.** Mass is the amount of matter in an object (kg) and stays the same everywhere. Weight is the gravitational force on it, W = mg (N), so it depends on g. A 90 kg astronaut has a mass of 90 kg on both Earth and the Moon, but weighs about 882 N on Earth and about 146 N on the Moon (g ≈ 1.62 N kg⁻¹).
<!-- id: physics-1-2-8 -->

Explanation: Both definitions plus the correct units earn full marks.

**A544.** A. twice Earth's
<!-- id: physics-1-2-9 -->

Explanation: M = ρ(4/3)πR³, so g = GM/R² = (4/3)πGρR ∝ R. Doubling R doubles g.

**A545.** B. towards the centre of mass of the planet
<!-- id: physics-1-2-10 -->

Explanation: Gravity attracts a test mass towards the centre of mass, so the field points radially inwards.

**A546.** C. 298 N
<!-- id: physics-1-2-11 -->

Explanation: g_Mars = GM/R² ≈ 3.73 N kg⁻¹, so W = mg = 80 × 3.73 ≈ 298 N. 784 N is her weight on Earth.

**A547.** g ∝ M/R² = 4/2² = 1, so g_X = 9.8 N kg⁻¹, the same as Earth.
<!-- id: physics-1-2-12 -->

Explanation: Use ratios rather than full values.

**A548.** D. a stronger gravitational field
<!-- id: physics-1-2-13 -->

Explanation: The density of field lines shows field strength; closer lines mean a stronger field.

**A549.** A. the gravitational force per unit mass on a small test mass placed at that point
<!-- id: physics-1-2-14 -->

Explanation: g = F/m, the force per kilogram on a test mass, measured in N kg⁻¹.

**A550.** B. 2.64 × 10⁶ m
<!-- id: physics-1-2-15 -->

Explanation: g ∝ 1/r², so r = √2 R = 9.01×10⁶ m and the altitude is r − R ≈ 2.64×10⁶ m. 9.01×10⁶ m is the radius, not the altitude.

**A551.** The force on a test mass m at distance r is F = GMm/r². Field strength is force per unit mass, g = F/m = (GMm/r²)/m = GM/r².
<!-- id: physics-1-2-16 -->

Explanation: State the definition g = F/m and show that m cancels.

**A552.** C. zero
<!-- id: physics-1-2-17 -->

Explanation: At the midpoint, fields of equal size point in opposite directions and cancel.

**A553.** D. parallel, equally spaced and pointing downwards
<!-- id: physics-1-2-18 -->

Explanation: A uniform field has the same strength and direction everywhere, shown by parallel, equally spaced lines.

**A554.** A. they and the station are both in free fall, accelerating at the same rate towards Earth
<!-- id: physics-1-2-19 -->

Explanation: g at the ISS is about 8.7 N kg⁻¹; the astronauts feel weightless because everything falls together, so there is no normal force.

**A555.** M = gR²/G = 3.7 × (3.39×10⁶)² / 6.67×10⁻¹¹ ≈ 6.4 × 10²³ kg.
<!-- id: physics-1-2-20 -->

Explanation: This is approximately the mass of Mars.

**A556.** B. 10 kg
<!-- id: physics-1-2-21 -->

Explanation: Mass is the amount of matter and does not change with location; only weight (mg) changes.

**A557.** C. 0.224 N kg⁻¹
<!-- id: physics-1-2-22 -->

Explanation: g = GM/r² = 3.98×10¹⁴ / (4.22×10⁷)² ≈ 0.224 N kg⁻¹.

**A558.** D. 1/9
<!-- id: physics-1-2-23 -->

Explanation: g ∝ 1/r², so at 3R it is (1/3)² = 1/9 of the surface value. Note that r is measured from the centre, not from the surface.

**A559.** Earth is slightly flattened (an oblate spheroid), so the poles are closer to Earth's centre than the equator. Because g ∝ 1/r², g is larger at the poles. (Earth's rotation also reduces the effective g at the equator.)
<!-- id: physics-1-2-24 -->

Explanation: One clearly explained reason is enough.

**A560.** g_Earth = GM_E/r² = 3.98×10¹⁴ / (3.0×10⁸)² ≈ 4.42×10⁻³ N kg⁻¹ towards Earth. g_Moon = GM_M/r² = 4.90×10¹² / (8.4×10⁷)² ≈ 6.95×10⁻⁴ N kg⁻¹ towards the Moon. Net ≈ 3.7×10⁻³ N kg⁻¹ towards Earth.
<!-- id: physics-1-2-25 -->

Explanation: The fields point in opposite directions, so subtract them.

**A561.** D. 4.9 N/kg
<!-- id: physics-1-2-c1 -->

Explanation: g = GM/r²: 2M ÷ (2r)² = 2/4 = 1/2 of Earth's value, so 9.8 ÷ 2 = 4.9 N/kg.

#### Satellites, orbits and Kepler's law

**A562.** A. 7.67 × 10³ m s⁻¹
<!-- id: physics-1-3-1 -->

Explanation: v = √(GM/r) = √(3.98×10¹⁴ / 6.77×10⁶) ≈ 7.67×10³ m s⁻¹. Omitting the square root gives 5.88×10⁷.

**A563.** B. 92 minutes
<!-- id: physics-1-3-2 -->

Explanation: T = 2πr/v = 2π × 6.77×10⁶ / 7.67×10³ ≈ 5.55×10³ s ≈ 92 min. Leaving out the 2 gives about 46 min.

**A564.** C. 4.22 × 10⁷ m
<!-- id: physics-1-3-3 -->

Explanation: r = ∛(GMT²/4π²) = ∛(3.98×10¹⁴ × 86400² / 4π²) ≈ 4.22×10⁷ m. 3.58×10⁷ m is the altitude above the surface, not the radius.

**A565.** GMm/r² = mv²/r and v = 2πr/T. Substituting: GM/r² = 4π²r/T², which rearranges to T² = 4π²r³/(GM).
<!-- id: physics-1-3-4 -->

Explanation: Key steps: equate the forces, substitute v = 2πr/T, rearrange.

**A566.** D. 8 times as long
<!-- id: physics-1-3-5 -->

Explanation: T² ∝ r³, so T ∝ r^(3/2) = 4^(3/2) = 8.

**A567.** A. the same speed and period
<!-- id: physics-1-3-6 -->

Explanation: v = √(GM/r) and T = 2π√(r³/GM) depend only on the central mass and the radius, not on the satellite's mass.

**A568.** B. the gravitational force, directed towards Earth's centre
<!-- id: physics-1-3-7 -->

Explanation: Gravity provides the centripetal force; the velocity changes direction, so the net force is not zero. There is no outward 'centrifugal' force in the inertial frame.

**A569.** T = 6000 s. r = ∛(GMT²/4π²) = ∛(3.98×10¹⁴ × 6000² / 4π²) ≈ 7.13×10⁶ m. Altitude = 7.13×10⁶ − 6.37×10⁶ ≈ 7.6×10⁵ m (about 760 km).
<!-- id: physics-1-3-8 -->

Explanation: Convert minutes to seconds and subtract Earth's radius at the end.

**A570.** C. lower
<!-- id: physics-1-3-9 -->

Explanation: v = √(GM/r), so a larger radius gives a smaller orbital speed.

**A571.** D. Above the equator, period of 24 hours, orbiting in the same direction as Earth's rotation
<!-- id: physics-1-3-10 -->

Explanation: To stay above one point, the satellite must match Earth's rotation: equatorial, 24 h period, same direction.

**A572.** A. the gravitational field strength at its orbital radius
<!-- id: physics-1-3-11 -->

Explanation: The only force is gravity, so a = F/m = GM/r² = g at that radius.

**A573.** A geostationary satellite stays above the same point on the equator because its 24-hour period matches Earth's rotation. Ground dishes can stay pointed at one fixed spot without tracking, giving continuous coverage of a large region.
<!-- id: physics-1-3-12 -->

Explanation: Link the 24 h period and equatorial orbit to a fixed position in the sky.

**A574.** B. 27.4 days
<!-- id: physics-1-3-13 -->

Explanation: T = 2π√(r³/GM) = 2π√((3.84×10⁸)³ / 3.98×10¹⁴) ≈ 2.37×10⁶ s ≈ 27.4 days.

**A575.** C. 6.4 × 10²³ kg
<!-- id: physics-1-3-14 -->

Explanation: M = 4π²r³/(GT²) = 4π² × (9.38×10⁶)³ / (6.67×10⁻¹¹ × (2.76×10⁴)²) ≈ 6.4×10²³ kg. Leaving out 4π² gives 1.6×10²².

**A576.** D. 1/√2
<!-- id: physics-1-3-15 -->

Explanation: v ∝ 1/√r, so doubling r multiplies v by 1/√2 ≈ 0.71.

**A577.** T_M/T_E = (r_M/r_E)^(3/2) = (2.28/1.50)^(3/2) = 1.52^1.5 ≈ 1.87, so T_Mars ≈ 1.87 years.
<!-- id: physics-1-3-16 -->

Explanation: Using a ratio with Earth avoids needing the Sun's mass.

**A578.** A. zero, because the force is always perpendicular to the velocity
<!-- id: physics-1-3-17 -->

Explanation: In a circular orbit gravity is always perpendicular to the displacement, so it does no work and the speed stays constant.

**A579.** B. GMm/(2r)
<!-- id: physics-1-3-18 -->

Explanation: mv²/r = GMm/r² gives mv² = GMm/r, so KE = ½mv² = GMm/(2r).

**A580.** C. 2 : 1
<!-- id: physics-1-3-19 -->

Explanation: v ∝ 1/√r, so v_A/v_B = √(4r/r) = 2.

**A581.** T = 27.3 × 86400 = 2.36×10⁶ s. v = 2πr/T = 2π × 3.84×10⁸ / 2.36×10⁶ ≈ 1.02×10³ m s⁻¹.
<!-- id: physics-1-3-20 -->

Explanation: Convert days to seconds first.

**A582.** D. 27 : 1
<!-- id: physics-1-3-21 -->

Explanation: T ∝ r^(3/2) = 9^(3/2) = 27.

**A583.** A. 2.66 × 10⁷ m
<!-- id: physics-1-3-22 -->

Explanation: r ∝ T^(2/3), so r = 4.22×10⁷ × (1/2)^(2/3) ≈ 2.66×10⁷ m. Simply halving gives 2.11×10⁷ m.

**A584.** B. the astronaut and the scale are falling together, so there is no normal force between them
<!-- id: physics-1-3-23 -->

Explanation: Apparent weight is the normal force. In free fall everything accelerates at g, so the normal force is zero.

**A585.** M = 4π²r³/(GT²) = 4π² × (1.50×10¹¹)³ / (6.67×10⁻¹¹ × (3.16×10⁷)²) ≈ 2.0×10³⁰ kg.
