Explanation: A systematic error shifts all values equally, so it reduces accuracy while leaving the spread (precision) unchanged.

**A1304.** C. reduce the effect of random errors on the mean, but not change any systematic error
<!-- id: physics-8-3-15 -->

Explanation: More trials improve the reliability of the mean against random scatter, but a systematic bias remains in every reading.

**A1305.** D. No effect, because the offset cancels when the initial temperature is subtracted from the final
<!-- id: physics-8-3-16 -->

Explanation: Both readings are 1.5 °C too high, so their difference is unaffected.

**A1306.** A. All speeds are 25% too high, a systematic error
<!-- id: physics-8-3-17 -->

Explanation: Speed is proportional to the entered length, and 5.0 ÷ 4.0 = 1.25, so every speed is 25% too high.

**A1307.** B. Random errors mainly affect precision; systematic errors mainly affect accuracy
<!-- id: physics-8-3-18 -->

Explanation: Scatter (random error) reduces precision, while a consistent bias (systematic error) moves results away from the true value.

**A1308.** Random errors cause unpredictable scatter of readings either side of the true value, e.g. variation in reaction time when using a stopwatch. Systematic errors shift every reading in the same direction by a consistent amount, e.g. a balance with a zero error or a miscalibrated thermometer.
<!-- id: physics-8-3-19 -->

Explanation: A clear definition and a valid example for each are needed.

**A1309.** The accepted value lies outside the range 9.37 to 9.47 m/s², so random error alone cannot explain the difference and a systematic error is likely. For example, measuring the length only to the top of the bob (making L too short) would make g consistently too low.
<!-- id: physics-8-3-20 -->

Explanation: Compare the accepted value with the uncertainty range, then suggest a plausible systematic error in the right direction.

**A1310.** A systematic error affects every reading in the same direction by the same amount, so the mean is shifted by the same amount. Averaging only reduces errors that vary randomly above and below the true value.
<!-- id: physics-8-3-21 -->

Explanation: Key idea: systematic errors do not cancel.

**A1311.** True diameter = 1.27 − 0.03 = 1.24 mm.
<!-- id: physics-8-3-22 -->

Explanation: Correct a zero error by subtracting the zero reading from every measurement.

**A1312.** 3.10 s is the outlier. Mean of the remaining values = (2.31 + 2.28 + 2.35 + 2.30) ÷ 4 = 9.24 ÷ 4 = 2.31 s.
<!-- id: physics-8-3-23 -->

Explanation: The outlier should be checked, noted and excluded rather than deleted silently.

**A1313.** It is too vague. Genuine mistakes (such as misreading a scale) should be avoided or the reading repeated, and are not part of the error analysis. The report should instead identify specific random or systematic errors and their effect on the results.
<!-- id: physics-8-3-24 -->

Explanation: Show that you understand the difference between mistakes and experimental errors.

**A1314.** This is a systematic error: friction always opposes motion, so the measured acceleration will be consistently less than g sin θ. It could be reduced by friction-compensating the track (tilting it slightly until the trolley moves at constant velocity) or including friction in the model.
<!-- id: physics-8-3-25 -->

Explanation: State the error type, the direction of the effect and a reasonable fix.

**A1315.** B. It causes a systematic error that repeating measurements cannot reduce
<!-- id: physics-8-3-c1 -->

Explanation: A zero offset shifts every reading by the same amount. Averaging does not remove it; correcting the zero does.

#### Graphing and linearisation

**A1316.** A. The independent variable
<!-- id: physics-8-4-1 -->

Explanation: The independent variable goes on the x-axis and the dependent variable on the y-axis.

**A1317.** B. T² against L
<!-- id: physics-8-4-2 -->

Explanation: Squaring gives T² = (4π²/g)L, which is linear in L with a gradient of 4π²/g.

**A1318.** C. 9.9 m/s²
<!-- id: physics-8-4-3 -->

Explanation: Gradient = 4π²/g, so g = 4π² ÷ 4.0 = 9.87 ≈ 9.9 m/s².

**A1319.** D. I against 1/r²
<!-- id: physics-8-4-4 -->

Explanation: If I = k/r², a graph of I against 1/r² is a straight line through the origin with gradient k.

**A1320.** A. F against v², gradient m/r
<!-- id: physics-8-4-5 -->

Explanation: F is directly proportional to v², with constant of proportionality m/r.

**A1321.** B. 25 Ω
<!-- id: physics-8-4-6 -->

Explanation: Gradient = I/V = 1/R, so R = 1 ÷ 0.040 = 25 Ω.

**A1322.** C. A straight line with points scattered roughly evenly on either side, not necessarily passing through every point or the origin
<!-- id: physics-8-4-7 -->

Explanation: A line of best fit represents the overall trend and balances the scatter of the points.

**A1323.** D. a systematic error in the measurements
<!-- id: physics-8-4-8 -->

Explanation: A constant offset in every measurement shifts the line, giving a non-zero intercept.

**A1324.** A. The threshold frequency
<!-- id: physics-8-4-9 -->

Explanation: Since E_k = hf − φ, E_k = 0 when f = f₀, the threshold frequency.

**A1325.** B. 3.0 m/s²
<!-- id: physics-8-4-10 -->

Explanation: With u = 0, s = ½at², so the gradient = a/2 and a = 2 × 1.5 = 3.0 m/s².

**A1326.** C. λ against 1/p
<!-- id: physics-8-4-11 -->

Explanation: Since λ = h × (1/p), a graph of λ against 1/p is linear with gradient h.

**A1327.** D. The elastic potential energy stored in the spring
<!-- id: physics-8-4-12 -->

Explanation: Work done = area under the F–x graph, which equals the elastic potential energy stored.

**A1328.** A. To show the uncertainty in each plotted value
<!-- id: physics-8-4-13 -->

Explanation: Error bars show the range within which each true value is likely to lie.

**A1329.** B. Estimating the uncertainty in the gradient
<!-- id: physics-8-4-14 -->

Explanation: Half the difference between the maximum and minimum gradients gives an estimate of the gradient's uncertainty.

**A1330.** C. 4.0
<!-- id: physics-8-4-15 -->

Explanation: Gradient = (2.05 − 0.45) ÷ (0.50 − 0.10) = 1.60 ÷ 0.40 = 4.0.

**A1331.** D. T² against r³
<!-- id: physics-8-4-16 -->

Explanation: T² is directly proportional to r³, with gradient 4π²/GM.

**A1332.** A. 6.0 × 10²⁴ kg
<!-- id: physics-8-4-17 -->

Explanation: M = 4π² ÷ (G × gradient) = 39.48 ÷ (6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 9.9 × 10⁻¹⁴) ≈ 6.0 × 10²⁴ kg.

**A1333.** B. The change in velocity
<!-- id: physics-8-4-18 -->

Explanation: Area = acceleration × time, which equals the change in velocity.

**A1334.** Square both sides: T² = (4π²/k)m. Plot T² (y-axis) against m (x-axis); the graph is a straight line through the origin with gradient 4π²/k, so k = 4π² ÷ gradient.
<!-- id: physics-8-4-19 -->

Explanation: State the variables plotted, the gradient expression and how k is obtained.

**A1335.** k = 4π² ÷ 0.20 = 39.5 ÷ 0.20 ≈ 2.0 × 10² N/m.
<!-- id: physics-8-4-20 -->

Explanation: Gradient = 4π²/k, so rearrange for k.

**A1336.** Measure I at several distances, then plot I against 1/r². If the graph is a straight line through the origin, then I ∝ 1/r² is supported; the gradient gives the constant of proportionality.
<!-- id: physics-8-4-21 -->

Explanation: The key is choosing axes that would give a straight line through the origin if the relationship holds.

**A1337.** Gradient = (5.2 + 4.6) ÷ 2 = 4.9, uncertainty = (5.2 − 4.6) ÷ 2 = 0.3. Gradient = 4.9 ± 0.3.
<!-- id: physics-8-4-22 -->

Explanation: Use the mean of the extreme gradients and half their difference.

**A1338.** φ = hf₀ = 4.14 × 10⁻¹⁵ × 5.5 × 10¹⁴ ≈ 2.3 eV.
<!-- id: physics-8-4-23 -->

Explanation: The x-intercept is the threshold frequency f₀ and the work function is φ = hf₀.

**A1339.** A straight line makes the relationship easy to recognise and test, deviations from the model are easy to see, and the gradient and intercept can be used to find physical constants (such as g or k) using all the data rather than one point.
<!-- id: physics-8-4-24 -->

Explanation: Mention both identifying the relationship and extracting constants from the gradient or intercept.

**A1340.** d = ½at², so the gradient = a/2. a = 2 × 0.60 = 1.2 m/s².
<!-- id: physics-8-4-25 -->

Explanation: Recognise that the gradient is half the acceleration.

**A1341.** Plot T² against L; g = 4π²/gradient ≈ 9.80 m/s²
<!-- id: physics-8-4-c1 -->

Explanation: Squaring gives T² = (4π²/g)L, a line through the origin with gradient 4π²/g. g = 4π² ÷ 4.03 ≈ 9.80 m/s².

### Challenge questions

#### Challenge set

**A1342.** C. They and the station are in free fall together, though gravity there is still about 90% of its surface value
<!-- id: physics-challenge-1 -->

Explanation: Gravity provides the centripetal force for the orbit. Since everything accelerates together, there is no normal force: apparent weightlessness.

**A1343.** B. 2.0 × 10⁵ m/s
<!-- id: physics-challenge-2 -->

Explanation: Undeflected means qE = qvB, so v = E/B = 2.0 × 10⁴ ÷ 0.10 = 2.0 × 10⁵ m/s, whatever the charge or mass.

**A1344.** About 15 m/s
<!-- id: physics-challenge-3 -->

Explanation: Friction provides the centripetal force: 0.60mg = mv²/r, so v = √(0.60 × 9.8 × 40) ≈ 15.3 m/s. The mass cancels.

**A1345.** About 6.3 m/s
<!-- id: physics-challenge-4 -->

Explanation: ½kx² = ½mv²: 1.0 J = ½ × 0.050 × v², so v = √40 ≈ 6.3 m/s.

**A1346.** D. 2.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ J
<!-- id: physics-challenge-5 -->

Explanation: KE = (γ − 1)mc² = 0.25 × 8.2 × 10⁻¹⁴ ≈ 2.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ J. The classical ½mv² gives 1.5 × 10⁻¹⁴ J, which is too small.

**A1347.** B. 0.10 A; 20 : 1
<!-- id: physics-challenge-6 -->

Explanation: Power in = power out: 240 × I = 12 × 2.0, so I = 0.10 A. Voltage ratio = turns ratio = 240 : 12 = 20 : 1.

**A1348.** About 1.7 × 10⁻¹⁰ m; it is similar to the atomic spacing, so diffraction is noticeable
<!-- id: physics-challenge-7 -->

Explanation: p = √(2mqV) = √(2 × 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ × 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 54) ≈ 3.97 × 10⁻²⁴ kg m/s. λ = h/p ≈ 1.7 × 10⁻¹⁰ m, so λ/w ≈ 1.

**A1349.** A. 2.00 ± 0.02 s
<!-- id: physics-challenge-8 -->

Explanation: Dividing by an exact number divides the value and the absolute uncertainty: 2.00 ± 0.02 s (1% either way).

**A1350.** The falling magnet changes the flux through the copper, inducing eddy currents. By Lenz's law their fields oppose the motion, slowing the magnet. Gravitational potential energy becomes heat in the copper, not just kinetic energy.
<!-- id: physics-challenge-9 -->

Explanation: Plastic is an insulator, so no current is induced. A full answer names induction, Lenz's law and the energy transfer.

**A1351.** About 3.1 × 10¹⁰ J; g is not constant over this distance (it falls to g/4 at 2R)
<!-- id: physics-challenge-10 -->

Explanation: ΔU = GMm(1/R − 1/2R) = GMm/(2R) = 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 5.98 × 10²⁴ × 1000 ÷ (2 × 6.37 × 10⁶) ≈ 3.1 × 10¹⁰ J. mgh with g = 9.8 would give about 6.2 × 10¹⁰ J, double the answer.

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**A1352.** Satellites in orbit
<!-- id: physics-x1 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** 8.21 N kg⁻¹ *(2 marks)*

1. Measure the orbital radius from Earth's centre: r = 6.37 × 10⁶ + 6.00 × 10⁵ = 6.97 × 10⁶ m. *(1 mark)*
2. \(g = \dfrac{GM}{r^2} = \dfrac{6.67 \times 10^{-11} \times 5.98 \times 10^{24}}{(6.97 \times 10^{6})^2}\) = 8.21 N kg⁻¹. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using 600 km as the radius. Distances in Newton's law of gravitation are measured from the centre of Earth.

Topic: Gravitational field strength (physics-1-2)

**b.** 7.56 × 10³ m s⁻¹ *(2 marks)*

1. Gravity provides the centripetal force: \(\dfrac{GMm}{r^2} = \dfrac{mv^2}{r}\), so \(v = \sqrt{\dfrac{GM}{r}}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(v = \sqrt{\dfrac{6.67 \times 10^{-11} \times 5.98 \times 10^{24}}{6.97 \times 10^{6}}}\) = 7.56 × 10³ m s⁻¹. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Including the satellite's mass in the calculation. It cancels, so the orbital speed does not depend on the satellite's mass.

Topic: Satellites, orbits and Kepler's law (physics-1-3)

**c.** 96.5 minutes *(2 marks)*

1. \(T = \dfrac{2\pi r}{v} = \dfrac{2\pi \times 6.97 \times 10^{6}}{7.56 \times 10^{3}}\) = 5.79 × 10³ s. *(1 mark)*
2. 5.79 × 10³ s ÷ 60 = 96.5 minutes. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Leaving the answer in seconds. The question asks for minutes.

Topic: Satellites, orbits and Kepler's law (physics-1-3)

**d.** The astronaut and the satellite are both in free fall towards Earth with the same acceleration, so the floor does not push on the astronaut. With no normal force, the astronaut's apparent weight is zero. *(2 marks)*

1. Gravity still acts on the astronaut and provides the centripetal force for the orbit. *(1 mark)*
2. The astronaut and satellite fall together with the same acceleration, so the floor exerts no normal force. With no normal force the apparent weight is zero, which is felt as weightlessness. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying there is no gravity in orbit. At 600 km gravity is still about 84% as strong as at the surface; the astronaut is in free fall, not free of gravity.

Topic: Newton's laws and forces (physics-3-1)

**e.** 4.23 × 10⁷ m *(2 marks)*

1. For a circular orbit, \(r^3 = \dfrac{GMT^2}{4\pi^2}\), with T = 24.0 × 3600 = 8.64 × 10⁴ s. *(1 mark)*
2. Substitute: \[r^3 = \frac{6.67 \times 10^{-11} \times 5.98 \times 10^{24} \times (8.64 \times 10^{4})^2}{4\pi^2} = 7.54 \times 10^{22}\ \text{m}^3\] so r = 4.23 × 10⁷ m. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Leaving T in hours. Use seconds so that every quantity is in SI units.

Topic: Satellites, orbits and Kepler's law (physics-1-3)

**f.** 0.200 m *(1 mark)*

1. λ = c ÷ f = 3.00 × 10⁸ ÷ 1.50 × 10⁹ = 0.200 m. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Treating GHz as 10⁶ Hz. Giga means 10⁹.

Topic: Wave properties and electromagnetic waves (physics-6-1)

**A1353.** Electrons: accelerated, turned and diffracted
<!-- id: physics-x2 -->
*Multi-part question · 13 marks*

**a.** E = 1.0 × 10⁵ V m⁻¹; F = 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁴ N *(2 marks)*

1. E = V ÷ d = 2.00 × 10³ ÷ 0.020 = 1.0 × 10⁵ V m⁻¹. *(1 mark)*
2. F = qE = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 1.0 × 10⁵ = 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁴ N. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using d = 2.0 instead of 0.020. Convert centimetres to metres before using E = V ÷ d.

Topic: Coulomb's law and electric fields (physics-2-1)

**b.** 3.20 × 10⁻¹⁶ J *(1 mark)*

1. The work done on the electron by the field is W = qV = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 2.00 × 10³ = 3.20 × 10⁻¹⁶ J, and all of it becomes kinetic energy. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Mixing up kilovolts and electronvolts. Each electron gains 2.00 keV, which is 2.00 × 10³ × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ J.

Topic: Coulomb's law and electric fields (physics-2-1)

**c.** ½mv² = 3.20 × 10⁻¹⁶ J, so v = √(2 × 3.20 × 10⁻¹⁶ ÷ 9.1 × 10⁻³¹) = 2.65 × 10⁷ m s⁻¹. *(2 marks)*

1. Set the kinetic energy equal to the work done: ½mv² = 3.20 × 10⁻¹⁶ J. *(1 mark)*
2. \(v = \sqrt{\dfrac{2 \times 3.20 \times 10^{-16}}{9.1 \times 10^{-31}}} = \sqrt{7.03 \times 10^{14}}\) = 2.65 × 10⁷ m s⁻¹. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Leaving out the ½ in ½mv², which gives 1.88 × 10⁷ m s⁻¹, too small by a factor of √2.

Topic: Momentum, impulse, work and energy (physics-3-4)

**d.** 3.0 × 10⁻² m (3.0 cm) *(2 marks)*

1. The magnetic force provides the centripetal force: \(qvB = \dfrac{mv^2}{r}\), so \(r = \dfrac{mv}{qB}\). *(1 mark)*
2. \(r = \dfrac{9.1 \times 10^{-31} \times 2.65 \times 10^{7}}{1.60 \times 10^{-19} \times 5.00 \times 10^{-3}}\) = 3.0 × 10⁻² m. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Treating mT as 10⁻⁶ T. Milli means 10⁻³, so 5.00 mT = 5.00 × 10⁻³ T.

Topic: Forces on moving charges and currents (physics-2-3)

**e.** The magnetic force is always at right angles to the electron's velocity, so it does no work. The kinetic energy, and so the speed, stays constant; only the direction of motion changes. *(2 marks)*

1. The force qvB acts at right angles to the velocity at every instant. *(1 mark)*
2. A force at right angles to the motion does no work, so the kinetic energy and the speed stay constant. The force only changes the direction, which gives circular motion. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying there is no force because the field is perpendicular to the motion. The force is largest when v is perpendicular to B; it changes the direction, not the speed.

Topic: Forces on moving charges and currents (physics-2-3)

**f.i.** 2.75 × 10⁻¹¹ m *(2 marks)*

1. p = mv = 9.1 × 10⁻³¹ × 2.65 × 10⁷ = 2.41 × 10⁻²³ kg m s⁻¹. *(1 mark)*
2. λ = h ÷ p = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ ÷ 2.41 × 10⁻²³ = 2.75 × 10⁻¹¹ m. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using λ = hc ÷ E, which applies to photons. For a particle with mass, use λ = h ÷ p.

Topic: De Broglie wavelength and diffraction (physics-7-2)

**f.ii.** 45.2 keV *(2 marks)*

1. The same pattern means the same wavelength, so E = hc ÷ λ = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ × 3.00 × 10⁸ ÷ 2.75 × 10⁻¹¹ = 7.23 × 10⁻¹⁵ J. *(1 mark)*
2. In electronvolts: 7.23 × 10⁻¹⁵ ÷ 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 4.52 × 10⁴ eV = 45.2 keV. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving the electrons' energy, 2.00 keV. A photon and an electron with the same wavelength have very different energies.

Topic: Wave-particle duality of light (physics-7-4)

**A1354.** From generator to town
<!-- id: physics-x3 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** 0.020 Wb *(1 mark)*

1. The flux falls from BA to zero: ΔΦ = BA = 0.40 × 0.050 = 0.020 Wb. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Multiplying by the number of turns here. Flux is per turn; the number of turns enters Faraday's law.

Topic: Magnetic flux and Faraday's law (physics-5-1)

**b.** 160 V *(2 marks)*

1. Faraday's law: ε = N ΔΦ ÷ Δt. *(1 mark)*
2. ε = 200 × 0.020 ÷ 0.025 = 160 V. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Forgetting the factor N = 200, which gives 0.80 V.

Topic: Magnetic flux and Faraday's law (physics-5-1)

**c.** The induced current flows in a direction that creates a magnetic field opposing the change in flux. *(1 mark)*

1. The induced current creates a magnetic field that opposes the change in magnetic flux through the coil. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the induced current opposes the magnetic field. It opposes the change in flux.

Topic: Lenz's law and induced currents (physics-5-2)

**d.** 50 A *(1 mark)*

1. I = P ÷ V = 250 000 ÷ 5000 = 50 A. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using V = IR with 5000 V and 4.0 Ω. The 5.00 kV is the transmission voltage, not the voltage drop across the lines.

Topic: Transformers and transmission losses (physics-5-4)

**e.** 10 kW *(2 marks)*

1. P_loss = I²R. *(1 mark)*
2. P_loss = 50² × 4.0 = 10 000 W = 10 kW. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using P = V²/R with the 5.00 kV supply voltage, which gives a huge, wrong value.

Topic: Transformers and transmission losses (physics-5-4)

**f.** 100 W; the current is 10 times smaller, and power loss depends on current squared. *(2 marks)*

1. New current: I = 250 000 ÷ 50 000 = 5.0 A, so P_loss = 5.0² × 4.0 = 100 W. *(1 mark)*
2. Raising the voltage tenfold lowers the current tenfold; since P_loss = I²R, the loss falls by a factor of 100. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the loss falls by a factor of 10. The loss depends on the square of the current.

Topic: Transformers and transmission losses (physics-5-4)

**g.** 7.07 kV *(1 mark)*

1. V_peak = √2 × V_RMS = √2 × 5.00 = 7.07 kV. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Dividing by √2. The peak is larger than the RMS value.

Topic: Generators, alternators and AC/RMS (physics-5-3)

**A1355.** Green light: waves and photons
<!-- id: physics-x4 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** 4.26 mm *(2 marks)*

1. Δx = λL ÷ d. *(1 mark)*
2. Δx = 532 × 10⁻⁹ × 2.00 ÷ 0.250 × 10⁻³ = 4.26 × 10⁻³ m = 4.26 mm. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Leaving the slit separation in millimetres. Convert 0.250 mm to 0.250 × 10⁻³ m.

Topic: Interference and Young's double slit (physics-6-2)

**b.** The path difference from the two slits is an odd number of half wavelengths, so the waves arrive out of phase and interfere destructively. *(2 marks)*

1. At a dark fringe the path difference is (n + ½)λ. *(1 mark)*
2. The waves from the two slits arrive out of phase (crest meets trough) and cancel: destructive interference. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the light is blocked by the barrier. Dark fringes come from interference, which supports the wave model.

Topic: Interference and Young's double slit (physics-6-2)

**c.** The fringes move further apart. *(1 mark)*

1. Red light has a longer wavelength, and Δx ∝ λ, so the spacing increases. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the spacing decreases because red light has lower energy. Spacing depends on wavelength.

Topic: Interference and Young's double slit (physics-6-2)

**d.** 2.33 eV *(2 marks)*

1. E = hc ÷ λ. *(1 mark)*
2. E = 4.14 × 10⁻¹⁵ × 3.00 × 10⁸ ÷ 532 × 10⁻⁹ = 2.33 eV. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using h = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ J s and then calling the answer eV. Match the units of h to the units you want.

Topic: Photoelectric effect and photons (physics-7-1)

**e.** 0.23 eV *(1 mark)*

1. E_k max = hf − φ = 2.33 − 2.10 = 0.23 eV. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Adding the work function. It is the energy needed to release the electron, so it is subtracted.

Topic: Photoelectric effect and photons (physics-7-1)

**f.** More photoelectrons are emitted, but their maximum kinetic energy is unchanged. *(2 marks)*

1. Doubling the intensity doubles the number of photons per second, so more electrons are emitted each second. *(1 mark)*
2. Each electron still absorbs one photon of the same energy, so the maximum kinetic energy does not change. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying brighter light gives faster electrons, as the wave model predicts. The photoelectric effect shows energy depends on frequency, not intensity.

Topic: Photoelectric effect and photons (physics-7-1)

**A1356.** Challenge: electrons as particles and waves
<!-- id: physics-cx1 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** 3.2 × 10⁻¹⁶ J *(1 mark)*

1. KE = qV = 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 2000 = 3.2 × 10⁻¹⁶ J. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving the answer in eV without converting to joules.

Topic: Particle accelerators and the synchrotron (physics-2-4)

**b.** 2.7 × 10⁷ m/s *(2 marks)*

1. ½mv² = 3.2 × 10⁻¹⁶ J. *(1 mark)*
2. v = √(2 × 3.2 × 10⁻¹⁶ ÷ 9.11 × 10⁻³¹) ≈ 2.7 × 10⁷ m/s. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Forgetting the square root.

Topic: Particle accelerators and the synchrotron (physics-2-4)

**c.** v ≈ 0.09c, so γ ≈ 1.004 and relativistic effects are below 1%. *(1 mark)*

1. v/c ≈ 0.088, giving γ ≈ 1.004, a negligible correction. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying relativity only applies to spaceships.

Topic: Time dilation and Lorentz factor (physics-4-2)

**d.** About 7.5 × 10⁻² m *(2 marks)*

1. qvB = mv²/r, so r = mv/(qB). *(1 mark)*
2. r = 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ × 2.65 × 10⁷ ÷ (1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 2.0 × 10⁻³) ≈ 0.075 m. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using 2.0 T instead of 2.0 × 10⁻³ T.

Topic: Forces on moving charges and currents (physics-2-3)

**e.** The magnetic force is always perpendicular to the velocity, so it does no work. *(1 mark)*

1. Force perpendicular to motion: no work is done, so the kinetic energy is constant. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying there is no force on the electrons.

Topic: Forces on moving charges and currents (physics-2-3)

**f.** About 2.7 × 10⁻¹¹ m *(2 marks)*

1. p = mv = 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ × 2.65 × 10⁷ = 2.4 × 10⁻²³ kg m/s. *(1 mark)*
2. λ = h/p = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ ÷ 2.4 × 10⁻²³ ≈ 2.7 × 10⁻¹¹ m. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using λ = hc/E, which is only for photons.

Topic: De Broglie wavelength and diffraction (physics-7-2)

**g.** The patterns have the same spacing because the wavelengths are equal. Each X-ray photon has E = hc/λ ≈ 7.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ J (about 45 keV), much more than the electron's 2 keV. *(2 marks)*

1. Same wavelength gives the same diffraction pattern. *(1 mark)*
2. Photon energy hc/λ ≈ 7.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ J, over 20 times the electron's KE. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Assuming equal wavelengths mean equal energies.

Topic: Wave-particle duality of light (physics-7-4)

**A1357.** Challenge: from wind turbine to town
<!-- id: physics-cx2 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** 160 V *(2 marks)*

1. ΔΦ = BA = 0.40 × 0.020 = 8.0 × 10⁻³ Wb in a quarter period, Δt = 1 ÷ (4 × 50) = 0.0050 s. *(1 mark)*
2. emf = NΔΦ/Δt = 100 × 8.0 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.0050 = 160 V. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using a full period for Δt.

Topic: Magnetic flux and Faraday's law (physics-5-1)

**b.** Both double. *(2 marks)*

1. Peak emf doubles, since the flux changes twice as fast. *(1 mark)*
2. Frequency doubles to 100 Hz. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying only the frequency changes.

Topic: Generators, alternators and AC/RMS (physics-5-3)

**c.** About 1 : 96 *(1 mark)*

1. N(p) : N(s) = 690 : 66 000 ≈ 1 : 96. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Inverting the ratio for a step-up transformer.

Topic: Transformers and transmission losses (physics-5-4)

**d.** 30 A; 7.3 kW; 242 V *(3 marks)*

1. I = P/V = 2.0 × 10⁶ ÷ 66 000 ≈ 30.3 A. *(1 mark)*
2. P(loss) = I²R = 30.3² × 8.0 ≈ 7.3 × 10³ W. *(1 mark)*
3. V(drop) = IR = 30.3 × 8.0 ≈ 242 V. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using P = V²/R with 66 kV, which treats the whole line voltage as dropped across the line.

Topic: Transformers and transmission losses (physics-5-4)

**e.** I ≈ 2900 A; I²R ≈ 67 MW, far more than the 2.0 MW produced, and IR ≈ 23 kV is more than the supply voltage. *(2 marks)*

1. I = 2.0 × 10⁶ ÷ 690 ≈ 2.9 × 10³ A. *(1 mark)*
2. I²R ≈ 6.7 × 10⁷ W (or a 23 kV drop), which is impossible, so almost no power would reach the town. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Concluding only that losses are 'larger', without numbers.

Topic: Transformers and transmission losses (physics-5-4)

**f.** Steady DC gives constant flux in the core, so no emf is induced in the secondary. *(1 mark)*

1. A transformer needs a changing flux; DC gives a constant flux. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying DC is too weak.

Topic: Lenz's law and induced currents (physics-5-2)
